Nonlocal nonlinear diffusion equations. Smoothing effects, Green functions, and functional inequalities
Matteo Bonforte, Jørgen Endal

Abstract
. We establish boundedness estimates for solutions of generalized porous medium equations of the form ∂ t u + ( − 𝔏 ) ​ [ u m ] = 0 in ℝ N × ( 0 , T ) , \partial_{t}u+(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]=0\quad\quad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(0,T)$}, where m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 and − 𝔏 -\mathfrak{L} is a linear, symmetric, and nonnegative operator. The wide class of operators we consider includes, but is not limited to, Lévy operators. Our quantitative estimates take the form of precise L 1 L^{1} – L ∞ L^{\infty} -smoothing effects and absolute bounds, and their proofs are based on the interplay between a dual formulation of the problem and estimates on the Green function of − 𝔏 -\mathfrak{L} and I − 𝔏 I-\mathfrak{L} . In the linear case m = 1 m=1 , it is well-known that the L 1 L^{1} – L ∞ L^{\infty} -smoothing effect, or ultracontractivity, is equivalent to Nash inequalities. This is also equivalent to heat kernel estimates, which imply the Green function estimates that represent a key ingredient in our techniques. We establish a similar scenario in the nonlinear setting m > 1 m>1 . First, we can show that operators for which ultracontractivity holds, also provide L 1 L^{1} – L ∞ L^{\infty} -smoothing effects in the nonlinear case. The converse implication is not true in general. A counterexample is given by 0 0 -order Lévy operators like − 𝔏 = I − J ∗ -\mathfrak{L}=I-J\ast . They do not regularize when m = 1 m=1 , but we show that surprisingly enough they do so when m > 1 m>1 , due to the convex nonlinearity. This reveals a striking property of nonlinear equations: the nonlinearity allows for better regularizing properties, almost independently of the linear operator. Finally, we show that smoothing effects, both linear and nonlinear, imply families of inequalities of Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev type, and we explore equivalences both in the linear and nonlinear settings through the application of the Moser iteration.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35K55, 35K65, 35A01, 35B45, 35R09, 35R11.

1.
Introduction and main results

In this paper, we consider solutions of generalized porous medium equations
[
115
]
:

(GPME)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]=0\qquad\qquad&\text{in $Q_{T}:=\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(0,T)$,}\\
u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}\qquad\qquad&\text{on $\mathbb{R}^{N}$,}\end{cases}

where
m\geq 1
,
T>0
,
0\leq u_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and the operator
-\mathfrak{L}
is at least linear, symmetric, nonnegative
1
1

1

And moreover, densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Basically, we need the comparison principle and
L^{p}
-decay to hold for solutions of (
GPME
). We refer the reader to Appendix
C
for further information. Note that the terminology “Dirichlet operator” appears in the literature also as “sub-Markovian operator”. This property is expressing the fact that the operator has to be order preserving.
, and includes Lévy operators
2
2

2

That is, operators which are nonnegative at any global nonnegative maximum (usually called the positive maximum principle), see e.g.
[
51
]
. When
c>0
, there is (strong) absorption in (
GPME
).
defined for
\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
c\geq 0
as

(1.1)

c\psi(x)-\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}a_{ij}\partial_{x_{i}x_{j}}^{2}\psi(x)-\underbrace{P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}}\big(\psi(x+z)-\psi(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(z)}_{=:\mathcal{L}^{\mu}[\psi](x)}

where the real matrix
[a_{ij}]_{i,j=1,\ldots,N}
is nonnegative and symmetric,
P.V.
is the Cauchy principal value, and:

\displaystyle\mu\text{ is a nonnegative symmetric Radon measure on
}\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}\text{ satisfying}

\displaystyle\int_{|z|\leq 1}|z|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(z)+\int_{|z|>1}1\,\mathrm{d}\mu(z)<\infty.

Important examples are the Laplacian, the fractional Laplacian, sum of onedimensional fractional Laplacians, and so-called convolution type or
0
-order operators given as
-\mathfrak{L}=I-J\ast
where
J\geq 0
satisfies
\|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=1
.

Boundedness estimates are the first step on the way to further regularity properties. This was exploited in e.g.
[
5
]
(cf. Theorem 2.2 in
[
66
]
),
[
60
, Section 7]
,
[
33
]
,
[
57
, Theorem 1.2]
,
[
116
, Theorem 1.2]
,
[
58
, Theorem 1.1]
, and
[
38
, Theorem 1.2]
. It is also an important estimate in obtaining uniqueness in
L^{1}
for very weak solutions of (
GPME
), see e.g.
[
39
,
63
,
62
]
. We will therefore focus on such estimates in this paper.

It is well-known since the works of Bénilan
[
11
]
and Véron
[
117
]
that the parabolic equation
\partial_{t}u-\Delta[\varphi(u)]=0
enjoys
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing when
\varphi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\varphi^{\prime}(r)\geq C|r|^{m-1}
(see also
[
116
, Theorem 8.2]
in the case of the fractional Laplacian
-\mathfrak{L}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, and
[
114
]
in the standard Laplacian case). Let us therefore fix
\varphi(r)=|r|^{m-1}r
. In the linear case (
m=1
), the standard heat equation and the fractional heat equation still enjoy
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing
[
8
,
31
]
, but there are cases in which the operator is too weak to ensure such estimates. This can e.g. be seen for the convolution type operators
-\mathfrak{L}=I-J\ast
(cf.
[
2
, Theorem 1.4 and Lemma 1.6]
), where the solutions are as smooth as the initial data. Hence, when the nonlinearity cannot help, the operator needs to be strong enough to provide bounded solutions. One of our main concerns is therefore the following question:

Which operators
\mathfrak{L}
produce bounded solutions of (
GPME
)?

To provide an answer to this intriguing question we will extend the so-called Green function method to a wide class of operators. Such a method was developed in a series of papers
[
34
,
35
,
22
,
24
,
23
,
14
,
15
,
28
]
both for operators on bounded domains and on manifolds, including the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. The key tool is having at disposal good estimates for the kernel of
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}
, i.e. the Green function
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}
. Now, applying the inverse operator on each side of the PDE in (
GPME
) yields the so-called dual equation

0=(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\partial_{t}u]+(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]]=\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}\ast\partial_{t}u+u^{m}.

Another essential ingredient is the so-called Bénilan-Crandall (time-monotonicity) estimate

\partial_{t}u(\cdot,t)\geq-\frac{u(\cdot,t)}{(m-1)t}\qquad\text{in $\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$,}

which is a weak version of the fact that the map
t\mapsto t^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u(\cdot,t)
is nondecreasing. This is well-known to be a consequence of the time-scaling and comparison principle for (
GPME
), cf.
[
13
,
114
]
.
3
3

3

The estimate is purely nonlinear since it degenerates when
m=1
. However, the stronger Aronson-Bénilan estimate
[
4
]
do hold for the linear case as well, but it relies on the operator itself having space-scaling. We refer the reader e.g. to
[
31
, Lemma 6.1]
and
[
59
, p. 1270]
. Thus, the Green function method can indeed hold for particular linear cases.
A combination of the above equations then gives the so-called fundamental upper bound or “almost representation formula”

(1.2)

u^{m}(x_{0},t)\leq\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})\ast_{x}u(\cdot,t).

The latter name is justified in the sense that the bound is similar to the one given by the representation formula (convolution with the heat kernel) in the linear case
m=1
, where the Green function
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})
is replaced by the heat kernel
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})
corresponding to the operator. In both cases, the boundedness estimates follows directly by applying various properties of
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})
and
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})
. Further details on the proofs can be found in Section
4
.

Our method allows to recover the well-known
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing result, cf. Theorem
3.1
and Figure
1
,

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-N\theta_{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}\qquad\text{for all $t>0$}

where
\alpha\in(0,2]
and
\theta_{\alpha}:=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
, is respectively valid for the Laplacian
(-\mathfrak{L})=(-\Delta)

[
113
,
114
,
69
]
and the fractional Laplacian
-\mathfrak{L}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}

[
59
]
(see also
[
50
,
47
]
with
p=2
). An immediate consequence of our approach is that also Lévy operators
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with
\mu
comparable to the measure of the fractional Laplacian enjoys the same estimate, see Lemma
7.8
. It is also interesting to note that we are able to treat operators whose Green functions have different power behaviours. Solutions of (
GPME
) with such operators satisfy

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-N\theta_{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}+t^{-N\theta_{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2\theta_{2}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$,}

see Theorem
3.3
. The examples treated in Section
7.2
are
-\mathfrak{L}=(-\Delta)+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, relativistic Schrödinger type operators
-\mathfrak{L}=(\kappa^{2}I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-\kappa^{\alpha}I
with
\kappa>0
, and
\mathfrak{L}
being the generator of a finite range isotropically symmetric
\alpha
-stable process in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with jumps of size larger than
1
removed. Finally, if
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
in (
1.2
), then we immediately obtain the following absolute bound (cf. Theorem
3.7
and Figure
3
):

(1.3)

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-1/(m-1)}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$.}

All operators on the form
I-\mathfrak{L}
provide such an estimate (Lemma
7.16
), and also the operator
-\mathfrak{L}=(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
corresponding to the Bessel potential (Lemma
7.21
)
4
4

4

This operator can be written as
I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
for
\mu
satisfying (
1
), i.e, on the form (
1.1
).
. They are furthermore examples of operators which have better boundedness properties in the nonlinear case than in the linear, see Remark
7.22
.

The Green function method requires the existence of an inverse
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}
with a kernel
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}
satisfying suitable estimates. This of course puts a restriction on the class of operators we are able to treat. To remedy this fact, we also develop another approach which consists in considering
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}
instead, i.e., the Green function associated with the resolvent operator
I-\mathfrak{L}
. In this case, the inverse always exists, and
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}
is at least as good as
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}
. By rewriting the PDE in (
GPME
) to
\partial_{t}u+(I-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]=u^{m}
, applying
(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}
, and using the time-monotonicity estimate (associated with
-\mathfrak{L}
), we obtain the following fundamental upper bound:

(1.4)

u^{m}(x_{0},t)\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{(m-1)t}+\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-1}\bigg)\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})\ast_{x}u(\cdot,t).

Hence, we see that we have to pay the price of treating an equation with the reaction term
u^{m}
, which we then have to reabsorb to be able to obtain good estimates in this case. However, note that we can split the estimation of (
1.4
) into two cases:

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-1}\leq\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-1}>\frac{1}{(m-1)t}.

In the first case, we already have the estimate
\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-1/(m-1)}
, while in the other

u^{m}(x_{0},t)\leq 2\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-1}\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})\ast_{x}u(\cdot,t),

from which we can deduce
\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
as long as
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
with
p\in(1,\infty)
. Hence, the fundamental upper bound (
1.4
) yields

(1.5)

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-1/(m-1)}+\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$,}

see Theorem
3.5
and Figure
2
. The operator
-\mathfrak{L}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(-\partial_{x_{i}x_{i}}^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
provides an important example in this case since
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}=\infty
(for some values of
\alpha
), while
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. We refer to Lemma
7.24
and Remark
7.25
for further information. Indeed, this is the first time the Green function method is able to treat this operator. We end this part by also mentioning that Lévy operators
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with
\mu
such that, for
\alpha\in(0,2)
and constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
,

(1.6)

\frac{C_{1}}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq 1}\leq\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq 1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\leq C_{3}\mathbf{1}_{|z|>1},

fall into this case (Lemma
7.26
). The latter fits with the “usual impression” in the PDE community regarding the least assumptions expected on nonlocal operators which would produce bounded solutions of (
GPME
). Nevertheless, we were not able to find such a result other places in the literature.

An alternative to the Green function method is the nowadays standard Moser iteration
[
95
,
96
]
, which requires the quadratic form associated to the operator to satisfy Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev (GNS) and Stroock-Varopoulos inequalities. In the case
-\mathfrak{L}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, we refer to
[
59
]
. We devote Section
6
to a further discussion on the connections between Green function estimates, heat kernel estimates, and functional inequalities like GNS. In the linear case
m=1
, it is well-known that
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing is equivalent with Nash inequalities (a subfamily of GNS)
[
97
]
, and moreover, equivalent with on-diagonal heat kernel
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}
estimates. We present those connections in Theorem
6.1
, where we also include—maybe the less-known—
equivalence
with Sobolev inequalities. Since we are interested in Green function estimates, we finally prove that the bound
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}\lesssim|x|^{-(N-\alpha)}
implies the Sobolev inequality. If the Green function exists, it is given by

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, off-diagonal heat kernel bounds is needed to give estimates on the Green function. In other words, we need more information on
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}
than what the previous equivalences give us. The linear panorama is more or less settled, and we move on to the nonlinear case
m>1
. Again,
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing is equivalent with a family of GNS inequalities which is now subcritical since
m>1
. The latter is somehow interesting in the sense that we need a weaker inequality, compared to the linear case, in order to prove
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing through the Moser iteration. However, this inequality is still equivalent with the Sobolev inequality by
[
7
]
. This is in contrast to the absolute bound which is equivalent to the Poincaré inequality! In general, the latter inequality can only give
L^{q}
–
L^{p}
-smoothing estimates through an iteration approach
[
75
,
77
]
, and somehow the Green function method then provides an improvement here (since we indeed reach
L^{\infty}
-estimates, cf. (
1.3
)). See the Figures
4
and
5
for the various connections.

The resolvent approach also offers further interesting insight. Since

\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

even poor on-diagonal heat kernel bounds for
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}
will give
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. This has at least two consequences: (i) Such estimates for
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}
imply both Green function bounds and also GNS inequalities, which furthermore imply that solutions of (
GPME
) (with
m>1
) are bounded whenever the Green function method and/or Moser iteration go through. (ii) If the operator is such that solutions of (
GPME
) with
m=1
are bounded, then also solutions of (
GPME
) with
m>1
are bounded (Theorem
3.9
). The last item corresponds to the “usual impression” in the PDE community, but again we were not able to find a good reference for such a statement. The first item provides a clear connection between the Green function method and the Moser iteration, but there are some rather simple on-diagonal bounds for which the algebra of the Moser iteration is hard to work out, while the Green function approach is more straightforward. Consider for example
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(x,t)\lesssim t^{-N/\alpha}\textup{e}^{t}
which corresponds to the Lévy operator
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with
\mu
satisfying (
1.6
). It is clear that the linear case has the estimate
\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-N/\alpha}\textup{e}^{t}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
, for a.e.
t>0
, but the unclear nonlinear case is in fact easily handled with the Green function method.

We have then reached our final task:

Can the nonlinear case provide bounded solutions in cases when the linear cannot?

The question has parallels to other equations for which regularizing effects only happen when the nonlinearity is strong enough. Take e.g. the scalar conservation law
\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}[f(u)]=0
. If
f(r)=r
, we are in the setting of the transport equation, and the solutions are as smooth as the initial data. Hence, the operator itself is not able to provide smoothing estimates. In the mentioned case,
f
needs to be so-called genuinely nonlinear to provide regularizing effects. A sufficient condition is
f^{\prime\prime}(r)>0
when
N=1
, and
f:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
defined as
f(r)=(u^{2}/2,u^{3}/3,\ldots,u^{N+1}/N+1)
when
N>1
.
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing can then be found in
[
104
]
, while other regularizing properties in e.g.
[
53
]
. In this context, we also mention
[
1
]
which treats e.g.
\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}[f(u)]-\Delta[u^{m}]=0
. Under some conditions on
f
, it is proven that properties like boundedness hold whenever it holds for
\partial_{t}u-\Delta[u^{m}]=0
.

We also found the answer to the above question by looking at operators which were too weak to provide boundedness estimates by themselves: the family
-\mathfrak{L}=I-J\ast
, mentioned earlier. Basically, the porous medium nonlinearity is so strong that we were even able to prove that solutions of (
GPME
) with those operators are bounded as in (
1.5
). Theorem
5.1
provides the rigorous statement, and what is interesting to note is that the proof resembles the Green function of the resolvent operator method.

Notation

Derivatives are denoted by
′
,
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}
, and
\partial_{x_{i}}
. We use standard notation for
L^{p}
,
W^{p,q}
, and
C_{\textup{b}}
. Moreover,
C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}
is the space of smooth functions with compact support,
C_{\textup{b}}^{\infty}
the space of smooth functions with bounded derivatives of all orders, and
C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
the space of measurable functions
\psi:[0,T]\to L_{\textup{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that
\psi(t)\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for all
t\in[0,T]
,
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{p}(K)}<\infty
, and
\|\psi(t)-\psi(s)\|_{L^{p}(K)}\to 0
when
t\to s
for all compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
t,s\in[0,T]
. In a similar way we also define
C([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
. Note that the notion of
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(0,T)\ni(x,t)\mapsto\psi(x,t)\in C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
is a subtle one. In fact, we mean that
\psi
has an a.e.-version which is continuous
[0,T]\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Let
f,g
be positive functions. The notation
f\lesssim g
or
f\gtrsim g
translates to
f\leq Cg
or
f\geq Cg
for some constant
C>0
. Hence,
f\eqsim g
is exactly that
f\lesssim g
and
f\gtrsim g
hold simultaneously. For
\alpha\in(0,2]
and
p\in[1,\infty)
, the quantity
(\alpha p+N(m-1))^{-1}
will either be denoted by
\theta_{p}
or
\theta_{\alpha}
, when there is no ambiguity. Finally, the following Young inequality is repeatedly used throughout the paper:

(1.7)

ab\leq\frac{1}{\vartheta}a^{\vartheta}+\frac{\vartheta-1}{\vartheta}b^{\frac{\vartheta}{\vartheta-1}},\qquad\text{where $a,b>0$ and $\vartheta>1$.}

2.
Assumptions and weak dual solutions

The spatial dimension is fixed to be
N\geq 3

5
5

5

The cases
N=1
and
N=2
are different and could fall out of our general setting. An example is the fractional Laplacian, where the condition
N>\alpha
plays an essential role in the form of the Green function. In this case we could consider
N\geq 1
, under the extra condition
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Also, the Green function of the standard Laplacian is sign-changing when
N=2
, and it falls out of our setting as it fails to satisfy assumption (
2
) below. Since we are dealing with Sobolev-type inequalities and their connection with smoothing effects, it is worth recalling that those inequalities tend to be different in dimension 1 and 2: For instance, functions of
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
are automatically bounded.

, and the assumptions on the data
(u_{0},m)
are:

(
\textup{H}_{u_{0}}
)

\displaystyle 0\leq u_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}).

(
\textup{H}_{m}
)

\displaystyle\text{The nonlinearity is $r\mapsto r^{m}$ for some fixed $m>1$}.

We will make repeated use of the Green functions (or fundamental solutions or potential kernels)
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
of the nonnegative operator
-\mathfrak{L}
and the positive operator
I-\mathfrak{L}
. A crucial assumption throughout the paper is therefore:

For the operator
A
, there exists a function
\mathbb{G}_{A}^{x_{0}}\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that:

0\leq\mathbb{G}_{A}^{x_{0}}=\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}(\cdot-x_{0})=\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}(x_{0}-\cdot)
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
A[\mathbb{G}_{A}^{x_{0}}]=\delta_{x_{0}}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Remark 2.1
.

The assumption
\mathbb{G}_{A}^{x_{0}}=\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}(\cdot-x_{0})
(possibly) excludes
x
-dependent operators. To include
x
-dependent operators, one would instead need
\mathbb{G}_{A}^{x_{0}}=\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}(\cdot,x_{0})
and
\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}(\cdot,x_{0})
continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{x_{0}\}
. In this case,
A^{-1}[f]
cannot be written as a convolution, but other than that, the proofs go through as before.

Appendix
D
provides a guide for checking (
2
) for specific operators. Let us just mention that when
A
is of the form (
1.1
) with a measure
\mu
satisfying (
1
), then
\mathbb{G}_{A}^{x_{0}}
satisfy the above under (possibly) some additional properties on the heat kernel associated with
A
. Moreover, for each such operator
A
, we have
A^{-1}
defined as

A^{-1}[f](y):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{G}_{A}^{y}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}(\cdot-y)f=\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}\ast f(y)=\mathbb{G}_{A}^{y}\ast f,

whenever that integral is convergent. The Green functions that will be used in this paper satisfy (with
C_{p},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},C_{1}>0
all independent of
x_{0}
) one of the following additional assumptions:

For all
R>0
, some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and some
\alpha\in(0,2]
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{1}R^{\alpha},&\\
\text{and for a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})$, }\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq K_{2}R^{-(N-\alpha)}.&\end{cases}

For all
R>0
, some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and some
\alpha\in(0,2]
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{1}R^{\alpha},&\\
\text{and for a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})$, }\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq\max\{K_{3},K_{2}R^{-(N-\alpha)}\}.&\end{cases}

For some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{1}<\infty.

For some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and some
p\in(1,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{p}<\infty.

Remark 2.2
.

(a)

Note that there is no ambiguity in assumption (
2
). Indeed, we cannot consider Green functions which are merely bounded around
x_{0}
since this would contradict the integrability condition.

(b)

We can view assumption (
2
) in two ways: (i) We think of
-\mathfrak{L}\mapsto I-\mathfrak{L}
in (
GPME
), i.e.,
c=1
in (
1.1
). (ii) We think of
-\mathfrak{L}
in (
GPME
), but we want to use the Green function of the resolvent of that operator, i.e.,
c=0
in (
1.1
). In both cases, if
-\mathfrak{L}
is such that the corresponding heat equation gives
L^{1}
-decay, then
\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 1
(see Lemma
7.16
). Hence, if we consider item (i), we are actually in the case (
2
).

(c)

For now, we just remark that the fractional Laplacian/Laplacian
-\mathfrak{L}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
with
\alpha\in(0,2]
satisfy (
2
) and (
2
)–(
2
), while
-\mathfrak{L}
of the full form (
1.1
) (with
c>0
) satisfies (
2
) and (
2
). Other important examples can be found in Section
7
, where heat kernel bounds and Fourier methods are used to obtain Green function bounds.

If we apply the inverse
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}
on each side of the PDE in (
GPME
), we get

0=(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\partial_{t}u]+(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]]=\partial_{t}\big(\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\ast_{x}u)+u^{m}.

We thus define a suitable class of solutions as the following:

Definition 2.1

(Weak dual solution)
.

We say that a nonnegative measurable function
u
is a
weak dual solution
of (
GPME
) if:

(i)

u\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
and
u^{m}\in L^{1}((0,T);L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
.

(ii)

For a.e.
0<\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq T
, and all
\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{1}([\tau_{1},\tau_{2}];L_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
,

(2.1)

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big((-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u]\partial_{t}\psi-u^{m}\psi\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u(\cdot,\tau_{2})](x)\psi(x,\tau_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u(\cdot,\tau_{1})](x)\psi(x,\tau_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

(iii)

u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Remark 2.3
.

(a)

We need to argue that
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u]\in C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
in order to make sense of the above definition. By using (
2
) and (
2
), we have

(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\mathbf{1}_{B_{r}(x_{0})}](x)=\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C

which implies that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u(\cdot,t)](x)\mathbf{1}_{B_{r}(x_{0})}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

for all
r>0
and all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. This makes us able to complete the argument as in Remark 2.1 in
[
15
]
. In the case of (
2
), we get the stronger

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u(\cdot,t)](x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

and hence,
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u]\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
.

(b)

Later, we will also use the the weak dual formulation for

\partial_{t}u+(I-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]=u^{m}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\partial_{t}u-\mathfrak{L}[u^{m}]=0.

Part (ii) of the above definition then looks like

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big((I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u]\partial_{t}\psi-u^{m}\psi+u^{m}(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi]\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u(\cdot,\tau_{2})](x)\psi(x,\tau_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u(\cdot,\tau_{1})](x)\psi(x,\tau_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

We again need
(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u]\in C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
. Since

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u(\cdot,t)](x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

which is finite by Remark
2.2
, we get the stronger
(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u]\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
.

(c)

Regarding uniqueness and very weak solutions. In many cases, weak dual solutions are very weak in the sense of
[
63
,
62
]
. For instance this happens when
C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}\subset\textup{dom}(-\mathfrak{L})
. A simple, and yet technical, proof follows by approximating
\mathfrak{L}[\phi]
by a sequence
\psi_{n}
of admissible test functions in (
2.1
). As a consequence, we can use the results of
[
63
,
62
]
to conclude existence and uniqueness of weak dual solutions in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
since we will show that they are a priori bounded. A general existence result for our purposes can be found in Proposition
4.1
.

3.
Statements of main boundedness results

We present some explicit estimates regarding instantaneous boundedness which rely on the assumptions (
2
)–(
2
). All of our results originate from what is often referred to as fundamental upper bounds, see Theorem
4.6
in Section
4
. These bounds provides an “almost representation formula” similar to the one given by convolution in the linear case (
m=1
).

\sum_{i=1}^{N}(-\partial_{x_{i}x_{i}}^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\text{ such that}\\
|z|^{-(N+\alpha)}\lesssim\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}\lesssim|z|^{-(N+\alpha)}\end{array}
\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-N\theta_{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}
Sobolev

Figure 1.
Operators that fall into the setting of Theorem
3.1
, see Section
7
. Note that the operator
\sum_{i=1}^{N}(-\partial_{x_{i}x_{i}}^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
actually enjoys Theorem
3.5
, but after a scaling argument, we can deduce the better estimate above (Remark
7.25
). According to Section
6
they should furthermore enjoy a Sobolev inequality.

3.1.
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing

We start with the assumptions (
2
) and (
2
) which impose the most structure. In effect, we deduce well-known results.

Theorem 3.1

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
.

Assume (

H

u
0

)–(
2
), and let
u
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
.

(a)

If (
2
) holds, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{C(m,\alpha,N)}{t^{N\theta_{\alpha}}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$},

where
\theta_{\alpha}:=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
,
C(m):=2^{\frac{m}{m-1}}
, and

C(m,\alpha,N):=2^{\frac{1}{m}}C(m)^{N\theta_{\alpha}}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}K_{1}^{(N-\alpha)\theta_{\alpha}}K_{2}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}.

(b)

If (
2
) holds, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{C(m,\alpha,N)}{t^{N\theta_{\alpha}}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<t\leq t_{0}$ a.e.,}\\
\frac{\tilde{C}(m)}{t^{\frac{1}{m}}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{1}{m}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $t>t_{0}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where
\tilde{C}(m):=(2m(m-1)^{-1}C(m)K_{3})^{1/m}
and

t_{0}:=2^{m}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{-(m-1)}C(m)K_{1}^{m}K_{2}^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}K_{3}^{-(\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}+(m-1))}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.

Remark 3.2
.

(a)

In this case, we can also get local smoothing estimates, see e.g. Proposition
4.23
.

(b)

Note that the estimate in Theorem
3.1
(a) is invariant under time-
and
space-scaling. Consider e.g. (
GPME
) with
-\mathfrak{L}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
. If
u
solves (
GPME
), then

u_{\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda}(x,t):=\kappa u(\Xi x,\Lambda t)\qquad\text{for all $\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda>0$}

also solves (
GPME
) as long as
\kappa^{m-1}\Xi^{\alpha}=\Lambda
. By inserting
u_{\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda}
into Theorem
3.1
(b), we see that the estimate remains the same since
\|u_{\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\kappa\Xi^{-N}\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
.

(c)

In a similar way, the second part of the estimate in Theorem
3.1
(b) is invariant under time-scaling (see Lemma
4.3
below). Even if that estimate might seem a bit unusual, it has appeared in the literature before, see e.g. Theorem 2.7 in
[
15
]
.

(d)

Observe also that the constant in front of both estimates blows up as
m\to 1^{+}
.

(e)

As expected,

\Big(\frac{1}{t}\Big)^{N\theta_{\alpha}}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>t_{0}$}

since the first estimate requires more assumptions at infinity.

We also include smoothing effects when (
2
) holds simultaneously for different
\alpha\in(0,2]
.

Theorem 3.3

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
.

Assume (

H

u
0

)–(
2
), and let
u
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. If (
2
) holds with
\alpha\in(0,2)
when
0<R\leq 1
and with
\alpha=2
when
R>1
, then:

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\tilde{C}(m)\begin{cases}t^{-N\theta_{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}&\qquad\text{if $0<t\leq\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}$ a.e.,}\\
t^{-N\theta_{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2\theta_{2}}&\qquad\text{if $t>\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where
\theta_{\alpha}=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
(defined for
\alpha\in(0,2]
) and

\tilde{C}(m):=2\Big((C(m)K_{1})^{\frac{m}{m-1}}+\frac{m}{m-1}C(m)K_{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}.

Remark 3.4
.

(a)

Note that
t=\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}
gives the bound
\tilde{C}(m)\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
in both cases.

(b)

We can of course combine other behaviours in a similar way, and as a rule of thumb one can say that
0<R\leq 1
gives small time behaviour while
R>1
gives large time behaviour.

I-J\ast
\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\text{ such that}\\
|z|^{-(N+\alpha)}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq 1}\lesssim\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}\lesssim|z|^{-(N+\alpha)}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq 1}\\
\quad\quad\quad\,\,\,\,\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{|z|>1}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{ll}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
\lesssim t^{-1/(m-1)}+\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{Weaker than}\\
\text{Sobolev}\end{array}

Figure 2.
Operators that fall into the setting of Theorem
3.5
, see Section
7
. It is clear that not all of these operators enjoy a Sobolev inequality since e.g.
I-J\ast
does not produce bounded solutions in the linear case. The general statement is therefore that they enjoy a functional inequality weaker than the Sobolev.

When we use the test function
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
, we lack “structure” in the sense that we do not assume, a priori, any precise behaviour of the Green function at zero nor at infinity. Still, we arrive at:

Theorem 3.5

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
.

Assume (

H

u
0

)–(
2
), and let
u
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. If (
2
) holds, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}2(m-1)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}&\qquad\text{if $0<t\leq t_{0}$ a.e.,}\\
2C(m)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}C_{p}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\qquad\text{if $t>t_{0}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where

t_{0}:=\frac{1}{m-1}\Big(C(m)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}C_{p}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\Big)^{-(m-1)}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}

and
C(m):=2(1+m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}
.

Remark 3.6
.

(a)

The time-scaling (see Lemma
4.3
below) ensures that the above estimate is of an invariant form.

(b)

Due to the “linear structure” of the fundamental upper bound in Theorem
4.6
(b), we cannot improve Theorem
3.5
even if we strengthen assumption (
2
) in the spirit of (
2
) or (
2
).

(c)

We would also like to refer the reader to
[
101
,
76
]
. The settings are respectively bounded domains or Riemannian manifolds, but (some of) the results have a flavour of the above estimate.

3.2.
Absolute bounds

We also include the especially well-behaved case when
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
is integrable.

Theorem 3.7

(Absolute bounds)
.

Assume (

H

u
0

)–(
2
), and let
u
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. If (
2
) holds, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\tilde{C}(m)t^{-1/(m-1)}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$},

where
\tilde{C}(m):=(2^{\frac{m}{m-1}}C_{1})^{1/(m-1)}
.

Remark 3.8
.

The above estimate immediately enjoys time-scaling (see Lemma
4.3
below).

I-\mathfrak{L}
(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-1/(m-1)}
Poincaré

Figure 3.
Operators that fall into the setting of Theorem
3.7
, see Section
7
. According to Section
6
they should furthermore enjoy a Poincaré inequality. Note that the Poincaré inequality is not strong enough to imply absolute bounds (only
L^{q}
–
L^{p}
-smoothing).

3.3.
Linear implies nonlinear

Since on-diagonal heat kernel bounds give
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing in the linear case, we are able to transfer such estimates to the nonlinear setting (
GPME
) by using
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}
as a test function, see the proof in Section
4.8
.

Theorem 3.9

(Linear implies nonlinear)
.

Assume
p\in(1,\infty)
and (

H

u
0

)–(
2
), and let
u
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with
m\geq 1
and initial data
u_{0}
. If the operator
-\mathfrak{L}
is such that
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
satisfy

0\leq\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C(t)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}C(t)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

then
u
is bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times[\tau,\infty)
, for a.e.
\tau>0
.

Remark 3.10
.

(a)

It is not possible to obtain that nonlinear implies linear since we construct a counterexample in Section
5
. There we find an operator for which a linear boundedness estimate does not hold, but a nonlinear do.

(b)

In the case of e.g.
-\mathfrak{L}=-\Delta
, the on-diagonal heat kernel bound gives

|u(x,t)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|u_{0}(x-y)|\mathbb{H}_{-\Delta}^{x_{0}}(y,t)\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim t^{-N/2}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(c)

By e.g.
[
94
, Theorem 8.16]
, linear operators satisfying the
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing is characterized by the Nash inequality when
C(t)\eqsim t^{-\gamma/(1-\gamma)}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Hence, the
p
needs to be restricted to
(1,\gamma/(2\gamma-1))
. That is, given
-\mathfrak{L}
for which
C(t)
is power-like, we can find
p
in that interval, and then, the result transfers to the nonlinear setting (
m>1
) in the sense that
u
solving (
GPME
) is bounded. See also the discussion in Section
6.1
.

4.
Proofs in the Green function setting

Starting from the fundamental upper bound, already mentioned in the introduction,

u^{m}(x_{0},t)\leq\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot-x_{0})\ast_{x}u(\cdot,t)=\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}(x-x_{0})u(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x.

one can indeed deduce
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing estimates. To motivate the proofs, let us provide the formal computations assuming (
2
). Split the integral over
\mathbb{R}^{N}
into
B_{R}(x_{0})
and
\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})
, then estimate each part:

u^{m}(x_{0},t)\leq\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\frac{1}{(m-1)t}K_{1}R^{\alpha}+\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\frac{1}{(m-1)t}K_{2}R^{-(N-\alpha)}

The Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=m
applied to the first term yields

\begin{split}\frac{1}{m}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m}+\frac{m-1}{m}\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)t}K_{1}R^{\alpha}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}.\end{split}

By taking the supremum, with respect to
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, on each side of the above inequality and using the
L^{1}
-decay of solutions, we get, for some constant
C>0
,

\begin{split}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m}&\leq\frac{1}{2}C^{m}\frac{R^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}}{t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\Big(1+\frac{t^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}}{R^{\frac{1}{(m-1)\theta_{\alpha}}}}\Big).\end{split}

We then have

R=\big(t^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\big)^{(m-1)\theta_{\alpha}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{C}{t^{N\theta_{\alpha}}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}.

4.1.
Properties of weak dual solutions

Our rigorous justification of the above computations starts with collecting some a priori results for weak dual solutions of (
GPME
), which will be a consequence of the existence theory—its proof is postponed to Appendix
E
. Note that the proof requires the operator
-\mathfrak{L}
to be in a specific class, and that this particular class is at least one of the requirements to ensure (
2
). A thorough discussion can be found in Appendix
D
.

Proposition 4.1

(Existence and a priori results)
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,
-\mathfrak{L}
is densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

)–(
2
), and (
2
)–(
2
).

(a)

There exists a weak dual solution
u
of (
GPME
) such that

0\leq u\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(Q_{T})\cap C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})).

(b)

Let
u,v
be weak dual solutions of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0},v_{0}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Then:

(i)

(Comparison)
If
u_{0}\leq v_{0}
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then
u\leq v
a.e. in
Q_{T}
.

(ii)

(
L^{p}
-decay)

\|u(\cdot,\tau_{2})\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|u(\cdot,\tau_{1})\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
and a.e.
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq T
.

Remark 4.2
.

(a)

If
u_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then Proposition
4.1
(b)(i)–(ii) hold also when
m=1
by approximation, and then also for
u_{0}\in TV(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

(b)

We provide no general uniqueness proof. However, the constructed solutions are unique by definition since they satisfy the comparison principle.

The following scaling property holds independently of the operator
\mathfrak{L}
:

Lemma 4.3

(Time-scaling)
.

Assume (

H
m

) and
\Lambda>0
. If
(x,t)\mapsto u(x,t)
solves (
GPME
) on
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(0,T)
, then

(x,t)\mapsto u_{\Lambda}(x,t):=\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u(x,\Lambda t)

solves (
GPME
) on
\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(0,\Lambda T)
for all
\Lambda>0
.

Proof.

Note that

\partial_{t}u_{\Lambda}(x,t)=\Lambda^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\partial_{t}u(x,\Lambda t)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{L}[u_{\Lambda}^{m}(\cdot,t)](x)=\Lambda^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\mathfrak{L}[u^{m}(\cdot,\Lambda t)](x),

and the proof is finished.
∎

Our proofs heavily relies on:

Proposition 4.4

(Time-monotonicity, Theorem 4 in
[
52
]
)
.

If
u
is a solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
, then the function
0<t\mapsto t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u^{m}(\cdot,t)
is nondecreasing for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Remark 4.5
.

Let us address the issue of defining exactly what we mean by the mapping “
0<t\mapsto t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u^{m}(\cdot,t)
is nondecreasing for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
”. Of course, if functions are continuous (in space and time) this is not an issue, but a priori
u
is merely integrable, hence some remarks are in order.

We shall make a systematical use of the following properties throughout the paper. Indeed, for functions
f\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})\subset L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(Q_{T})
, we have that:

(i)

If
(x_{0},t_{0})
is a Lebesgue point for
f
, then it is a Lebesgue point for
f^{m}
. This follows from the fact that
f
is essentially bounded.

(ii)

If
(x_{0},t_{0})
is a Lebesgue point for
f^{m}
, then it is also a Lebesgue point for
f
. This easily follows from Jensen inequality and from the fact that
m>1
, so that the power nonlinearity
f^{m}
is convex.

(iii)

We can always choose a version of
f
which is bounded at every point, and such that all points are Lebesgue points. Indeed, we know that the set of “non-Lebesgue points” has measure zero, as well as the set where the function is not bounded, hence we can just redefine the function at those points as: letting
Q_{R}(x,t)=B_{R}(x)\times[t-R,t+R]\subset Q_{T}
we define

f(x,t):=\lim_{R\to 0}\frac{1}{|Q_{R}(x,t)|}\int_{Q_{R}(x,t)}f(y,\tau)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

noticing that the latter integral is always finite, since
f\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})
. Hence in this case we have a version of
f
for which all points are Lebesgue points.

(iv)

Moreover, if
f\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
, we can choose a version such that
f:[0,T]\to L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a continuous mapping, so that for all
t\in[0,T]
,
t\mapsto f(\cdot,t)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Throughout the paper we will therefore fix a version of a solution
u\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})\cap C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
to the (
GPME
) such that all the above properties hold. These properties provide a precise meaning to the statements that we will often use in the proofs, in particular when we use a solution
u
evaluated at a Lebesgue point
(t,x)
, or at a Lebesgue point with respect to one variable, for instance a point
(\cdot,t)
for a.e.
t>0
or a point
(x,\cdot)
for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. This is clear in view of the fact that we can redefine
u\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})\cap C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
so that
all
space-time points are Lebesgue points, as explained above. This will happen for instance in the proof of Theorem
3.5
, and also in the fundamental upper bounds Theorem
4.6
and its consequences. Sometimes, one can also extend the validity of statements that hold “for a.e.
t>0
” to statements that hold “for all
t>0
”.

Since (
GPME
) enjoys time-scaling, the framework provided in
[
52
]
simplifies. In our setting, the proof only relies on the comparison principle. We thus include the argument, which we originally learned from Prof. Juan Luis Vázquez (see also
[
13
,
113
]
).

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

For all
\Lambda\geq 1
, we have
\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u_{0}\geq u_{0}
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Lemma
4.3
gives that
u_{\Lambda}
solves (
GPME
) with initial data
\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u_{0}
, and then the comparison principle (Theorem
4.1
(b)(i)) implies
u_{\Lambda}\geq u
for
(x,t)\in Q_{T}
and all
\Lambda\geq 1
. For any fixed
t>0
, choose

\Lambda:=\frac{t+h}{t}=1+\frac{h}{t}\qquad\text{for all $h\geq 0$.}

Then

u(x,t)\leq u_{\Lambda}(x,t)=\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u(x,\Lambda t)=\Big(\frac{t+h}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u(x,t+h).

We conclude by noting that
r\mapsto r^{m}
is increasing.
∎

4.2.
Reduction argument

Throughout, we fix
\tau_{*},T>0
such that
0<\tau_{*}<T
, and let
\tau\in(\tau_{*},T]
. We also consider the following sequence of approximations
\{u_{0,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

(4.1)

\begin{cases}0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})\text{ such that}\\
u_{0,n}\to u_{0}\text{ in $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ and}\\
u_{0,n}\to u_{0}\text{ a.e. in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ monotonically from below as $n\to\infty$.}\end{cases}

When we take
u_{0,n}
as initial data in (
GPME
), we denote the corresponding solutions by
u_{n}
, and they satisfy Proposition
4.1
, Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
4.4
.

4.3.
Fundamental upper bounds

This section is devoted to prove:

Theorem 4.6

(Fundamental upper bounds)
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

), and (
2
). If
u_{n}
is a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0,n}
, then:

(a)

Under assumptions (
2
)–(
2
), for a.e.
\tau_{*}>0
and all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq C(m)\frac{1}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
C(m):=2^{\frac{m}{m-1}}
.

(b)

Under assumption (
2
), for a.e.
\tau_{*}>0
and all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq C(m)\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

where

C(m):=2(1+m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lambda:=\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-1}>\frac{1}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}.

Remark 4.7
.

(a)

Theorem
4.6
(a) corresponds to equation (5.3) in
[
34
]
.

(b)

Theorem
4.6
(b) somehow corresponds to the mentioned equation (5.3) as well, however, the inequality has a “linear structure” due to the presence of
\lambda
.

We begin by choosing the test function in the weak dual formulation.

Lemma 4.8
.

Assume (
2
). Then there is a constant
C
depending on the Green function such that:

(a)

If (
2
) or (
2
) holds, then

\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi]\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C(\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})})\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}

(b)

If (
2
) or (
2
) holds, then, for
A=-\mathfrak{L}
or
A=I-\mathfrak{L}
respectively,

\|A^{-1}[\psi]\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}

Proof.

(a) To incorporate the assumptions (
2
)–(
2
), we split the integral over the sets
B_{R}(x)
and
\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x)
and change the variable
x-y\mapsto y
to obtain

\begin{split}|(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi](x)|&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x-y)|\psi(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int_{B_{R}(0)}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(y)|\psi(x-y)|\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(0)}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(y)|\psi(x-y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&\leq\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\int_{B_{R}(0)}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+C\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.\end{split}

The bound in
L^{\infty}
then follows.

(b) We simply use that
\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, see Remark
2.2
.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

), (
2
), and (
2
)–(
2
).
Let
0\leq\psi\in L_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
0\leq\Theta\in C_{\textup{b}}^{1}([\tau_{*},\tau])
. If
u_{n}
is a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0,n}
, then, for a.e.
\tau\in(\tau_{*},T]
,

(4.2)

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\Theta(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\Theta^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,t)](x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\Theta(\tau_{*})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})](x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\Theta(\tau)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)](x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Proof.

Define
\gamma(x,t):=\Theta(t)\psi(x)
.
Consider a sequence
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{\text{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times[\tau_{*},\tau])
(i.e., a mollifying sequence) such that
\psi_{k}\to\gamma
and
\partial_{t}\psi_{k}\to\partial_{t}\gamma
a.e. as
k\to\infty
.
By Definition
2.1
(with
\tau_{1}:=\tau_{*}
,
\tau_{2}:=\tau
, and
\psi=\psi_{k}
),

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)](x)\psi_{k}(x,\tau)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})]\psi_{k}(x,\tau_{*})\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big((-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}]\partial_{t}\psi_{k}-u_{n}^{m}\psi_{k}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\end{split}

holds for a.e.
\tau\in(\tau_{*},T]
. Since
u_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times[\tau_{*},\tau])
,
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}]\in C([\tau_{*},\tau];L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
, and
\psi_{k}
is compactly supported, we can take the limit in the above equality to get the result.
∎

Corollary 4.10

(Limit estimate 1)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.9
,
let
\psi
approximate
\delta_{x_{0}}
and choose
\Theta\equiv 1
. Then

\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

for all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Proof.

Since we choose
\Theta\equiv 1
, equation (
4.2
) becomes

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})](x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)](x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi](x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi](x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Now, fix
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and choose

\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{B_{1/k}(x_{0})}}{|B_{1/k}(x_{0})|}\in L_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}).

as a test function in the above equality. Since
u_{n}^{m}(\cdot,t)\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and by the definition of a Lebesgue point,

\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\to\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\,\mathrm{d}t\qquad\text{as $k\to\infty$.}

For the remaining two terms, the argument is bit more involved, but let us start with the simplest case, in which
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
satisfies (
2
). Since
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
is symmetric and integrable, we get

\begin{split}&\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}](x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\\
&=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)\bigg(\fint_{B_{1/k}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y-\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\\
&\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\fint_{B_{1/k}(x_{0})}|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(y-x)-\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x_{0}-x)|\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\fint_{B_{1/k}(x_{0})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(z)-\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(z+(x_{0}-y))|\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\sup_{|x_{0}-y|\leq 1/k}\|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}-\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(\cdot+(x_{0}-y))\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},\end{split}

which goes to zero as
k\to\infty
by the continuity of the
L^{1}
-translation.

In the case of (
2
) and (
2
), we still have that
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and hence,

(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}](x)=\frac{1}{|B_{1/k}(x_{0})|}\int_{B_{1/k}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\to\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x}(x_{0})=\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)

for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
as
k\to\infty
. However, we cannot simply apply the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem since the
L^{\infty}
-bound of
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}]
depends on
\|\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim k^{N}
coming from the estimate in
B_{R}(x_{0})
by Lemma
4.8
. We therefore split the integral over the sets
B_{R}(x_{0})
and
\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})
:

\begin{split}&\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}](x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\\
&=\bigg|\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau)(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}](x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau)\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\\
&\quad+\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau)(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}](x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau)\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\\
&=:I_{1}+I_{2}.\end{split}

The integral
I_{1}
can be handled more or less as for (
2
). Indeed, since
|x-x_{0}|\leq R
and
|x-y|\leq(3/2)R
(in the latter we assume that
k\geq 2/R
), we estimate
I_{1}
as

\begin{split}I_{1}&\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\fint_{B_{1/k}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{(3/2)R}(0)}|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(z)-\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(z+(x_{0}-y))|\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\times\\
&\quad\times\sup_{|x_{0}-y|\leq 1/k}\big\|\big(\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\mathbf{1}_{B_{(5/2)R}(0)}\big)-\big(\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\mathbf{1}_{B_{(5/2)R}(0)}\big)(\cdot+(x_{0}-y))\big\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},\end{split}

which goes to zero as
k\to\infty
by the continuity of the
L^{1}
-translation. To estimate
I_{2}
, we consider

\begin{split}I_{2}&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}|u_{n}(x,\tau)|\big|(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}](x)-\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Now, since
|x-x_{0}|\geq R
and
|y-x_{0}|\leq 1/k\leq(1/2)R
, we use the triangle inequality to get
|x-y|\geq|x-x_{0}|-1/k\geq(1/2)R
. Hence, both
(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi_{k}^{(x_{0})}](x)=\fint_{B_{1/k}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y
and
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)
are uniformly bounded in
k
by (
2
) and (
2
). The conclusion then follows by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem.
∎

In the case of
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
, we note that we can obtain a similar result as in Proposition
4.9
(see also Remark
2.3
(b)):

(4.3)

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\Theta(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big(\Theta^{\prime}(t)+u_{n}^{m-1}(x,t)\Theta(t)\big)u_{n}(x,t)(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[\psi](x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\Theta(\tau_{*})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})](x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\quad-\Theta(\tau)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(I-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[u_{n}(\cdot,\tau)](x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

To find a suitable
\Theta
, we need to fix
\tau_{*}>0
and
T(\lambda):=\tau_{*}+\frac{m}{\lambda(m-1)}>\tau_{*}
. The latter is denoted by
T
from now on.

Lemma 4.11
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and (

H
m

). Let
u_{n}
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0,n}
,
t\in[\tau_{*},T]
, and define

\Theta(t):=(T-t)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}.

Then
0\leq\Theta\in C_{\textup{b}}^{1}([\tau_{*},T])
and solves

\Theta^{\prime}(t)+u^{m-1}(x,t)\Theta(t)\leq 0\qquad\text{for a.e. $t\in[\tau_{*},T]$ and a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

Remark 4.12
.

(a)

In particular, the choice
\tau=T=\tau_{*}+\frac{m}{\lambda(m-1)}
will be used throughout the rest of the paper.

(b)

The exponent
\frac{m}{m-1}
is chosen to match the one of the time-monotonicity (Proposition
4.4
).

Proof of Lemma
4.11
.

A direct computation gives

\Theta^{\prime}(t)=\frac{m}{m-1}(T-t)^{\frac{m}{m-1}-1}(-1)=-\frac{m}{(m-1)(T-t)}\Theta(t).

By Proposition
4.1
(b)(ii) with
p=\infty
,

0\leq u_{n}^{m-1}(x,t)\leq\|u_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(\tau_{*},T))}^{m-1}\leq\lambda

and then

\Theta^{\prime}(t)+u_{n}^{m-1}(x,t)\Theta(t)\leq\Big(\lambda-\frac{m}{(m-1)(T-t)}\Big)\Theta(t)\leq 0,

where, in the last inequality, we used that
\lambda
is such that

\lambda<\frac{m}{(m-1)(T-t)}\qquad\text{for all $t\in[\tau_{*},T]$.}

This finished the proof.
∎

By following the proof of Corollary
4.10
as for the assumption (
2
), we get:

Corollary 4.13

(Limit estimate 2)
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

), (
2
), and (
2
). Let
u_{n}
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0,n}
, let
\psi
approximate
\delta_{x_{0}}
, and choose
\Theta
as in Lemma
4.11
. Then

\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\Theta(t)u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\Theta(\tau_{*})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

for all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Remark 4.14
.

We note that Corollaries
4.10
and
4.13
reveal that another proper functional setting is the one where

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,t)\mathbb{G}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$.}

We are ready to prove the fundamental upper bounds.

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

We begin the proof by noting the following consequence of Proposition
4.4
:
For a.e.
t\in[\tau_{*},\tau]
and all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

(4.4)

\tau_{*}^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\leq\tau^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau).

(a)
For a.e.
\tau\geq\tau_{*}>0
, we combine Corollary
4.10
and (
4.4
) to get

\begin{split}\tau_{*}^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\,\mathrm{d}t&\leq\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

or

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})&\leq\frac{1}{\tau_{*}^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\Bigg(\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\frac{1}{(m-1)\tau_{*}^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\Bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau_{*}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}-\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\Bigg)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Note that
t\mapsto t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}
is convex when
m>1
, and hence,

\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau_{*}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}-\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\geq\frac{1}{m-1}\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau}\bigg)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\big(\tau-\tau_{*}\big).

Moreover,

u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq\bigg(\frac{\tau}{\tau_{*}}\bigg)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\frac{1}{\tau-\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

We conclude by choosing
\tau=2\tau_{*}
.

(b)
For some fixed
T>\tau_{*}
, we combine Corollary
4.13
and (
4.4
) to get

\begin{split}\tau_{*}^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\frac{\Theta(t)}{t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\,\mathrm{d}t&\leq\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\frac{\Theta(t)}{t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\Theta(\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

or

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})&\leq\frac{(T-\tau_{*})^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}{\tau_{*}^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\Bigg(\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\frac{(\tau-t)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}{t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\Big(\frac{T-\tau_{*}}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\Bigg(\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\Big(\frac{\tau-t}{t}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\Big(1+\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)\left(\frac{T}{\tau_{*}}\right)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\frac{1}{T-\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

The last step follows from the estimate

\begin{split}\Big(\frac{T-\tau_{*}}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\Bigg(\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\Big(\frac{\tau-t}{t}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg)^{-1}\leq\Big(1+\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)\left(\frac{T}{\tau_{*}}\right)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\frac{1}{T-\tau_{*}}\end{split}

which can be proven as follows:

\begin{split}&\Big(\frac{\tau_{*}}{T-\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\Big(\frac{T-t}{t}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\Big(\frac{\tau_{*}}{T-\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\Big(\frac{T-t}{T}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\Big(\frac{\tau_{*}}{T}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\frac{1}{(T-\tau_{*})^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{T}\Big(T-t\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\Big(1+\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{-1}\Big(\frac{\tau_{*}}{T}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\frac{(T-\tau_{*})^{\frac{m}{m-1}+1}}{(T-\tau_{*})^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}.\end{split}

We thus have

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})&\leq\Big(1+\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)\left(\frac{T}{\tau_{*}}\right)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\frac{1}{T-\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

The choice
T=\tau_{*}+\frac{m}{\lambda(m-1)}
and the assumption
\lambda^{-1}<(m-1)\tau_{*}
readily give

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})&\leq\Big(1+\frac{m-1}{m}\Big)\left(1+\frac{m}{\lambda(m-1)\tau_{*}}\right)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\Big(2-\frac{1}{m}\Big)(1+m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq 2(1+m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

which is the desired result.
∎

4.4.
Boundedness under (
2
)

Recall that the fundamental upper bound (Theorem
4.6
(b)) was only valid when
\lambda>((m-1)\tau_{*})^{-1}
. Hence, we need to combine that case with
\lambda\leq((m-1)\tau_{*})^{-1}
to reach a finial conclusion. Under the latter assumption, however, we immediately have

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$.}

Let us therefore continue with
\lambda>((m-1)\tau_{*})^{-1}
.

Lemma 4.15

(
L^{q}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
be such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
and (
2
), we have that

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C(m)C_{p}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$.}

Proof.

By Theorem
4.6
(b), we get

u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq C(m)\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Now, take the essential supremum over
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
on both sides and use the Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=m/(m-1)>1
to get

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m}\leq\frac{m-1}{m}\lambda^{\frac{m}{m-1}}+\frac{1}{m}\bigg(C(m)\esssup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{m},

or, since
\lambda=\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-1}
,

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C(m)\esssup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

By assumption,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

and the result follows.
∎

So,
u_{n}(\cdot,t)
is in fact bounded whenever
u_{n}(\cdot,t)\in L^{q}
for some
q\in(1,\infty)
. We exploit this in the next result.

Lemma 4.16

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
be such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
and (
2
), we have that

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C(m)^{q}C_{p}^{q}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$.}

Proof.

We use the Hölder inequality in the proof of the Lemma
4.15
to get

\begin{split}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x&\leq\lambda\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\\
&\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{(m-1)q+q-1}{q}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{1}{q}}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.\end{split}

Now, the Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=mq>1
gives

\begin{split}&\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{mq-1}{q}}C(m)\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{1}{q}}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}}\\
&\leq\frac{mq-1}{mq}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}+\frac{1}{mq}C(m)^{mq}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}}^{m}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}}^{mq}.\end{split}

Combining the above yields

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}\leq C(m)^{mq}C_{p}^{mq}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}}^{m}.\qed

We sum up the results in the following theorem:

Proposition 4.17

(Smoothing effects)
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
be such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
and (
2
), we have that:

(a)

(
L^{q}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}+C(m)C_{p}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$.}

(b)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}+C(m)^{q}C_{p}^{q}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$.}

The above results are not invariant under time-scaling (Lemma
4.3
). We thus rewrite them in a proper form:

Proposition 4.18

(Scaling-invariant smoothing effects)
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
be such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
and (
2
), we have that:

(a)

(
L^{q}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}2((m-1)t)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<t\leq t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\\
2C(m)C_{p}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $t>t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where

t_{0,n}:=\frac{1}{m-1}\Big(C(m)C_{p}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\Big)^{-(m-1)}.

(b)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}2((m-1)t)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<t\leq t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\\
2C(m)^{q}C_{p}^{q}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $t>t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where

t_{0,n}:=\frac{1}{m-1}\Big(C(m)^{q}C_{p}^{q}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\Big)^{-(m-1)}.

Proof.

We only provide a proof for part (a) since part (b) is similar.

Proposition
4.17
(a) gives

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}+C(m)C_{p}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{q}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$,}

but this result is not respecting the time-scaling (Lemma
4.3
):

\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\Lambda\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\Lambda\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}+\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}C(m)C_{p}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\Lambda\tau_{*})\|_{L^{q}}.

By Proposition
4.1
(b)(ii) with
p=q
, we can optimize by requiring that

\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\Lambda\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}=C(m)C_{p}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{q}},\quad\text{or}\quad\Lambda\tau_{*}=\frac{1}{m-1}\Big(\frac{1}{C(m)C_{p}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{q}}}\Big)^{m-1}=:t_{0,n}.

We obtain that

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0,n})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2C(m)C_{p}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{q}}.

Now, if
0<t\leq t_{0,n}
, we use time-monotonicity (Lemma
4.4
)

u_{n}(\cdot,t)\leq\Big(\frac{t_{0,n}}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0,n})

to get

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}&\leq\Big(\frac{t_{0,n}}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0,n})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Big(\frac{t_{0,n}}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}2C(m)C_{p}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{q}}=2\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}.\end{split}

And, if
t>t_{0,n}
, we use Proposition
4.1
(b)(ii) with
p=\infty

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0,n})\|_{L^{\infty}}

to get

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0,n})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2C(m)C_{p}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{q}}.\qed

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

By the proof of Proposition
4.1
, we know that for every
u_{0,n}
, there is a unique mild solution
u_{n}
enjoying comparison and
L^{p}
-decay, and this solution is moreover a weak dual solution in the sense of Definition
2.1
. As a consequence, the
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing of Proposition
4.18
(b) holds for
u_{n}
.
By construction, we have that
0\leq u_{0,n}\leq u_{0,n+1}\leq u_{0}
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
(see (
4.1
)), so that Proposition
4.1
(b)(i) yields

0\leq u_{n}\leq u_{n+1}\qquad\text{a.e. in $Q_{T}$ for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$.}

By monotonicity, the pointwise limit of
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
always exists (possibly being
+\infty
on a set of measure zero), and we then define our candidate limit solution as

u(x,t):=\liminf_{n\to\infty}u_{n}(x,t).

Moreover, by the Fatou lemma and Proposition
4.1
(b)(ii), we immediately have that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

As a consequence, the set
\{(x,t)\in Q_{T}\,:\,u(x,t)=\infty\}
has measure zero, so that the convergence above holds a.e. in
Q_{T}
, and
0\leq u_{n}\leq u
a.e. in
Q_{T}
. Note that, for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and a.e.
t>0
, we have

u(x,t)=\liminf_{n\to\infty}u_{n}(x,t)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,.

As a consequence,
u
inherits from
u_{n}
the mentioned
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing effect of Proposition
4.18
(b) since (
4.1
) gives

\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\to\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\quad\text{and}\quad t_{0,n}\to t_{0}\qquad\text{as $n\to\infty$}.

It remains to check that the constructed limit
u
is indeed a weak dual solution. To that end, note that the regularity assumptions
u^{m}\in L^{1}((0,T);L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
and
u\in L^{\infty}((0,T);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
are straightforward consequences of the fact that
u\in L^{1}(Q_{T})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times[\tau,T))
for a.e.
\tau>0
. Moreover,
0\leq u_{n}\leq u_{n+1}\leq u
a.e. and
u_{n}\to u
a.e. as
n\to\infty
yield that a simple use of the monotone convergence theorem ensures that parts (ii) and (iii) of Definition
2.1
are true for
u
(the limit integrals are all finite). It only remains to prove that
u\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
. Let us begin with
t\in(0,T)
. We shall use that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
u_{n}\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
satisfies the following time-monotonicity estimate (cf. Proposition
4.4
):

t_{0}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u_{n}(x,t_{0})\leq t_{1}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u_{n}(x,t_{1}),\qquad\text{for a.e. $t_{1}\geq t_{0}>0$ and a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$,}

which can be rearranged to

u_{n}(x,t_{1})-u_{n}(x,t_{0})\geq\Big(\frac{t_{0}}{t_{1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}u_{n}(x,t_{0})-u_{n}(x,t_{0})=-\Big(1-\Big(\frac{t_{0}}{t_{1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\Big)u_{n}(x,t_{0}).

Now, recall that
|f|=2f^{-}+f=2(-\min\{f,0\})+f
, so that

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{1})-u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0})&\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big(u_{n}(x,t_{1})-u_{n}(x,t_{0})\big)^{-}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big(u_{n}(x,t_{1})-u_{n}(x,t_{0})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq 2\Big(1-\Big(\frac{t_{0}}{t_{1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\Big)\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}-\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\\
&\leq\frac{2\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}}{t_{1}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}}\big(t_{1}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}-t_{0}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\big),\end{split}

where we used that
u_{n}\geq 0
, the
L^{1}
-decay of
u_{n}
, and
u_{0,n}\leq u_{0}
. Changing the roles of
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
reveals that the estimate also holds when
0<t_{1}\leq t_{0}
, i.e.,

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{1})-u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{2\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}}{t_{1}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}}\big|t_{1}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}-t_{0}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\big|.

Moreover, since
u_{n}(\cdot,t)\to u(\cdot,t)
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
for a.e.
t>0
, we get that
|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{1})-u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0})|\to|u(\cdot,t_{1})-u(\cdot,t_{0})|
. Then a simple application of the Fatou lemma yields

\|u(\cdot,t_{1})-u(\cdot,t_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{1})-u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{2\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}}{t_{1}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}}\big|t_{1}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}-t_{0}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\big|,

so that
u\in C((0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
.

The continuity at
t=0
is a consequence of the triangle inequality. Indeed, for a.e.
t\in(0,T]
,

\begin{split}\|u(\cdot,t)-u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq\|u(\cdot,t)-u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)-u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\\
&\quad+\|u_{0,n}-u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.\end{split}

The last two terms go to zero as
n\to\infty
since
u_{n}\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
and by the assumption (
4.1
) on
u_{0,n}
. Finally, the first term goes to zero by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, noting that
|u(\cdot,t)-u_{n}(\cdot,t)|\leq 2|u(\cdot,t)|\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.
∎

Remark 4.19
.

Note that we never used any of the particular assumptions on the Green function (
2
)–(
2
) here. This will be important later when we want repeat the above argument in slightly different settings.

4.5.
Boundedness under (
2
) and (
2
)

Proposition 4.20

(Smoothing effects)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
, we have that:

(a)

If (
2
) holds, then

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{C(m,\alpha,N)}{\tau_{*}^{N\theta}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$},

where
\theta:=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
and

C(m,\alpha,N):=2^{\frac{1}{m}}C(m)^{N\theta}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{\alpha\theta}K_{1}^{(N-\alpha)\theta}K_{2}^{\alpha\theta}.

(b)

If (
2
) holds, then

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{C(m,\alpha,N)}{\tau_{*}^{N\theta}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<\tau_{*}\leq t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\\
\Big(\frac{\tilde{C}(m)}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{1}{m}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $\tau_{*}>t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where
\tilde{C}(m):=2mC(m)K_{3}
and

t_{0,n}:=2^{m}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{-(m-1)\theta}C(m)K_{1}^{m}K_{2}^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}K_{3}^{-(\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}+(m-1))}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.

The proof is based on the following intermediate results:

Proposition 4.21

(Local smoothing effects)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
, and that, for all
\rho>0
and all
\alpha\in(0,2]
,

\int_{B_{\rho}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{1}\rho^{\alpha}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{\rho}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty,

we get, for a.e.
\tau_{*}>0
, a.e.
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and all
0<\bar{R}<R<2\bar{R}
,

\begin{split}&\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}\\
&\leq AR^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}+\esssup_{x_{0}\in B_{R}(z)}\frac{m}{m-1}\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

where

A:=\Big(\frac{2C(m)}{\tau_{*}}K_{1}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}.

Remark 4.22
.

(a)

By Corollary
4.10
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{0,n}(x)\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

(b)

The above assumptions are analogous to the space
L_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
discussed in
[
91
]
.

Proof of Proposition
4.21
.

Fix
0<\bar{R}<R<2\bar{R}
. We split the integral in Theorem
4.6
(a) and use assumption (
2
) to obtain

\begin{split}&u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\\
