&\leq\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\quad+\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\Big(\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0}))}+\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0}))}\Big)\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}K_{1}R^{\alpha}\\
&\quad+\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

The Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=m
applied to the first term yields

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2m}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0}))}^{m}&+\frac{1}{2m}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0}))}^{m}\\
&+\frac{2^{1+\frac{1}{m-1}}(m-1)}{m}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}K_{1}R^{\alpha}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}.\end{split}

By taking the supremum on each side with respect to
x_{0}\in B_{\bar{R}}(z)
and using that

\esssup_{x_{0}\in B_{\bar{R}}(z)}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0}))}\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{2\bar{R}}(z))}\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3\bar{R}}(z))}

and

\esssup_{x_{0}\in B_{\bar{R}}(z)}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus B_{\bar{R}}(x_{0}))}\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R+\bar{R}}(z))}\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3\bar{R}}(z))},

we get

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}&\leq\frac{1}{m}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}+\frac{m-1}{m}AR^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\esssup_{x_{0}\in B_{R}(z)}\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

To conclude, we absorb the term
\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}
due to a classical lemma (cf. Lemma
A.2
).
∎

Proposition 4.23

(Local smoothing effects 2)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
, and for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and all
\bar{R}>0
small enough, we have that:

(a)

If (
2
) holds, then

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}\leq\frac{C(m,\alpha,N)}{\tau_{*}^{N\theta}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$},

where
\theta:=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
and

C(m,\alpha,N):=2^{\frac{1}{m}+(N-\alpha)\theta}C(m)^{N\theta}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{\alpha\theta}K_{1}^{(N-\alpha)\theta}K_{2}^{\alpha\theta}.

(b)

If (
2
) holds, then

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{C(m,\alpha,N)}{\tau_{*}^{N\theta}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<\tau_{*}\leq t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\\
\Big(\frac{\tilde{C}(m)}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{1}{m}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $\tau_{*}>t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where
\tilde{C}(m):=2mC(m)K_{3}
and

t_{0,n}:=2^{m}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{-(m-1)}C(m)K_{1}^{m}K_{2}^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}K_{3}^{-(\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}+(m-1))}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.

Proof.

(a) Recall that we fixed
0<\bar{R}<R<2\bar{R}
. Now, estimate

\esssup_{x_{0}\in B_{R}(z)}\frac{m}{m-1}\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

in Proposition
4.21
by using (
2
) to get

\frac{m}{m-1}\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}K_{2}R^{-(N-\alpha)}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=:BK_{2}R^{-(N-\alpha)}.

Optimizing in
R
gives

R=\Big(\frac{BK_{2}}{A}\Big)^{(m-1)\theta},

and

\begin{split}&\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}\\
&\leq 2A^{1-\alpha m\theta}(BK_{2})^{\alpha m\theta}\\
&=2^{1+(N-\alpha)m\theta}C(m)^{Nm\theta}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}\Big)^{\alpha m\theta}K_{1}^{(N-\alpha)m\theta}K_{2}^{\alpha m\theta}\frac{1}{\tau_{*}^{Nm\theta}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha m\theta}.\end{split}

(b) According to assumption (
2
), we have power-like behaviour of the Green function when

0<R\leq\Big(\frac{K_{2}}{K_{3}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{N-\alpha}},

and power-like behaviour around
x=x_{0}
and constant around
x\to\infty
when

R>\Big(\frac{K_{2}}{K_{3}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{N-\alpha}}.

Let us then first consider the case of small
R
. Following part (a), we have

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}\leq AR^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}+BK_{2}R^{-(N-\alpha)}.

Optimizing in
R
gives

R=\Big(\frac{BK_{2}}{A}\Big)^{(m-1)\theta},

and also the
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing of part (a). However, this can only hold when

\Big(\frac{BK_{2}}{A}\Big)^{(m-1)\theta}\leq\Big(\frac{K_{2}}{K_{3}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{N-\alpha}}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\tau_{*}\leq t_{0,n}.

Now, we turn our attention to the case of big
R
. Following part (a), we instead have

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}\leq AR^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-1}}+BK_{3}.

Optimizing in
R
gives

R=\Big(\frac{BK_{3}}{A}\Big)^{\frac{m-1}{\alpha m}},

and

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{R}}(z))}^{m}&\leq 2BK_{3}=\frac{2m}{m-1}C(m)K_{3}\frac{1}{\tau_{*}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.\end{split}

Similarly as in the case of small
R
, the above estimate can now only hold when
\tau_{*}>t_{0,n}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.20
.

We simply take the supremum over
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
3.1
follows as for Theorem
3.5
.

4.6.
Boundedness under combinations of (
2
)

Proposition 4.24

(Combined smoothing effects)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
, we have that:
If (
2
) holds with
\alpha\in(0,2)
when
0<R\leq 1
and with
\alpha=2
when
R>1
, then:

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\tilde{C}(m)\begin{cases}\tau_{*}^{-N\theta_{\alpha}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}&\qquad\text{if $0<\tau_{*}\leq\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}$ a.e.,}\\
\tau_{*}^{-N\theta_{2}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2\theta_{2}}&\qquad\text{if $\tau_{*}>\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where
\theta_{\alpha}=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
(defined for
\alpha\in(0,2]
) and

\tilde{C}(m):=2\Big((C(m)K_{1})^{\frac{m}{m-1}}+\frac{m}{m-1}C(m)K_{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}.

Proof.

Fix
0<R\leq 1
(to be determined). We split the integral in Theorem
4.6
(a) and use assumption (
2
) to obtain

\begin{split}&u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\\
&\leq\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}K_{1}R^{\alpha}+\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}K_{2}R^{-(N-\alpha)}.\end{split}

We then proceed as in the beginning of Section
4
to obtain

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{\tilde{C}(m)}{\tau_{*}^{N\theta_{\alpha}}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}

as long as

\big(\tau_{*}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\big)^{(m-1)\theta_{\alpha}}\leq 1\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\tau_{*}\leq\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.

Now, fix
R>1
(to be determined). By simply repeating the above calculations (replacing
\alpha
by
2
), the choice

R=\big(\tau_{*}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\big)^{(m-1)\theta_{2}}

gives

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{\tilde{C}(m)}{\tau_{*}^{N\theta_{2}}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2\theta_{2}}

as long as

\big(\tau_{*}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\big)^{(m-1)\theta_{2}}>1\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\tau_{*}>\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.\qed

The proof of Theorem
3.3
follows as for Theorem
3.5
.

4.7.
Boundedness under (
2
)

Proposition 4.25

(Absolute bounds)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
and (
2
), we have that

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\Big(\frac{C(m)C_{1}}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $\tau_{*}>0$.}

Proof.

By Theorem
4.6
(a), we get

u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq C(m)\frac{1}{\tau_{*}}\int u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}}.

Now, the Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=m
gives

\Big(1-\frac{1}{m}\Big)\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}\leq\frac{m-1}{m}\Big(\frac{C(m)C_{1}}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}},

and hence, the result follows.
∎

The proof of Theorem
3.7
follows as for Theorem
3.5
.

4.8.
Linear implies nonlinear

Proof of Theorem
3.9
.

Linear smoothing effects hold due to Theorem
6.1
below. As for the nonlinear case, to be in the setting of Theorem
3.5
, we only need to check that (
2
) holds. By Proposition
D.3
and the Minkowski inequality for integrals (cf. Theorem 2.4 in
[
94
]
),

(4.5)

\begin{split}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\Big(\textup{e}^{-t}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x,t)\Big)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\Big(\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x,t)\Big)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Finally,

\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\leq C(t)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}

completes the proof.
∎

5.
Boundedness results for
0
-order operators

We need some assumptions regarding
0
-order or nonsingular operators, i.e., operators of the form
-\mathfrak{L}=-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with
\,\mathrm{d}\mu=J\,\mathrm{d}z
where:

(
\textup{J}_{1}
)

J\geq 0
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, symmetric, and
\|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=1
.

(
\textup{J}_{2}
)

\displaystyle\|J\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{J,p}<\infty\textup{ for some $p\in(1,\infty]$.}

I.e., we consider convolution type operators
-\mathfrak{L}=I-J\ast
(which we will denote by
-\mathcal{L}^{J}
). The nonlinear equation (
GPME
) with such operators has been studied in e.g.
[
67
]
. Assumption (

J
2

) ensure that
J
is far away from being concentrated. Therefore, we cannot consider discrete measures
\mu
, and thus, operators like the discrete Laplacian. The smoothing takes the following form:

Theorem 5.1

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
.

Assume (

H

u
0

), (

H
m

), and
q=p/(p-1)\in[1,\infty)
, and let
u
be a very weak solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. If (

J
1

) and (

J
2

) hold, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}2(mqC(m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}})^{\frac{1}{m}}t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<t\leq t_{0}$ a.e.,}\\
2\Big(\frac{mC(m)}{m-1}C_{J,p}\Big)^{q}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $t>t_{0}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where
C(m):=2^{\frac{m}{m-1}}
and

t_{0}:=(mq)^{\frac{m-1}{m}}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}C_{J,p}\Big)^{-q(m-1)}C(m)^{1-q(m-1)}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.

Remark 5.2
.

The time-scaling (Lemma
4.3
) ensures that the above estimate is of the proper form.

The
0
-order or nonsingular operators have a particularly simple approach. In contrast to general singular Lévy operators in this paper which maps
W^{2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,
0
-order operators
-\mathfrak{L}=-\mathcal{L}^{J}
are well-defined for merely
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
-functions:

-\mathcal{L}^{J}:L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\qquad\text{for all $p\in[1,\infty]$.}

We then define very weak solutions:

Definition 5.1

(Very weak solution)
.

Assume
-\mathfrak{L}=-\mathcal{L}^{J}
. We say that a nonnegative measurable function
u
is a
very weak solution
of (
GPME
) if:

(i)

u\in L^{1}(Q_{T})\cap C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
and
u^{m}\in L^{1}(Q_{T})
.

(ii)

For a.e.
0<\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq T
, and all
\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}])
,

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big(u\partial_{t}\psi-(-\mathcal{L}^{J})[u^{m}]\psi\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u(x,\tau_{2})\psi(x,\tau_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u(x,\tau_{1})\psi(x,\tau_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

(iii)

u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Remark 5.3
.

For general Lévy operators, see (
1.1
), we need to put the operator on the test function instead.

We collect some known a priori results for (
GPME
) which will be useful in the proofs, see e.g. Theorem 2.3 in
[
62
]
.

Lemma 5.4

(Known a priori results)
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

), and
-\mathfrak{L}=-\mathcal{L}^{J}
.

(a)

There exists a unique very weak solution
u
of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
such that

0\leq u\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(Q_{T})\cap C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})).

(b)

Let
u,v
be very weak solutions of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0},v_{0}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Then:

(i)

(Comparison)
If
u_{0}\leq v_{0}
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then
u\leq v
a.e. in
Q_{T}
.

(ii)

(
L^{p}
-decay)

\|u(\cdot,\tau_{2})\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|u(\cdot,\tau_{1})\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
and a.e.
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq T
.

Remark 5.5
.

If
u_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then Lemma
5.4
(b)(i)–(ii) hold also when
m=1
by approximation, and then also for
u_{0}\in TV(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Again, we fix
\tau_{*},T>0
such that
0<\tau_{*}<T
, and let
\tau\in(\tau_{*},T]
. We also consider the following sequence of approximations
\{u_{0,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

\begin{cases}0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})\text{ such that}\\
u_{0,n}\to u_{0}\text{ in $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$, and}\\
u_{0,n}\to u_{0}\text{ a.e. in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ monotonically from below.}\end{cases}

When we take
u_{0,n}
as initial data in (
GPME
), we denote the corresponding solutions by
u_{n}
, and they satisfy Lemmas
5.4
,
4.3
and Proposition
4.4
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

),
-\mathfrak{L}=-\mathcal{L}^{J}
, and (

J
1

). Let
0\leq v\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
0\leq\Theta\in C_{\textup{b}}^{1}([\tau_{*},\tau])
. If
u_{n}
is a very weak solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0,n}
, then, for a.e.
\tau\in(\tau_{*},T]
,

(5.1)

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\Theta(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathcal{L}^{J})[u_{n}^{m}(\cdot,t)](x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\Theta^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,t)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\Theta(\tau_{*})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau_{*})v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\Theta(\tau)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}(x,\tau)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Corollary 5.7

(Limit estimate 3)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
5.6
,
let

v_{R}(x):=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{B(x_{0},R)}(x)}{|B(x_{0},R)|}\qquad\text{with $R>0$}

approximate
\delta_{x_{0}}
“
=\mathbb{G}_{I}^{x_{0}}
” and choose
\Theta\equiv 1
. Then

\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}(-\mathcal{L}^{J})[u_{n}^{m}(\cdot,t)](x_{0})\,\mathrm{d}t=u_{n}(x_{0},\tau_{*})-u_{n}(x_{0},\tau)

for all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Proof.

Simply apply the Lebesgue differentiation theorem as
R\to 0^{+}
in (
5.1
).
∎

Theorem 5.8

(Fundamental upper bound)
.

Assume
0\leq u_{0,n}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

),
-\mathfrak{L}=-\mathcal{L}^{J}
, and (

J
1

). If
u_{n}
is a very weak solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0,n}
, then, for a.e.
\tau_{*}>0
and all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq C(m)\bigg(\frac{u_{n}(x_{0},\tau_{*})}{\tau_{*}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg).\end{split}

where
J^{x_{0}}(x)=J(x-x_{0})
and
C(m)=2^{\frac{m}{m-1}}
.

Remark 5.9
.

This is a completely new result, but we see that
J^{x_{0}}
somehow takes the role of
\mathbb{G}^{x_{0}}
.

Proof of Theorem
5.8
.

We begin the proof by noting the following consequence of Proposition
4.4
:
For a.e.
t\in[\tau_{*},\tau]
and all Lebesgue points
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

(5.2)

\tau_{*}^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\leq\tau^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau).

We rearrange the result in Corollary
5.7
:

\begin{split}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},t)\,\mathrm{d}t&=u_{n}(x_{0},\tau_{*})-u_{n}(x_{0},\tau)+\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq u(x_{0},\tau_{*})+\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Arguing by time-monotonicity (
5.2
), as in the proof of Theorem
4.6
(a), leads to

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq\bigg(\frac{\tau}{\tau_{*}}\bigg)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\frac{1}{\tau-\tau_{*}}\bigg(u_{n}(x_{0},\tau_{*})+\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg).\end{split}

Choose
\tau=2\tau_{*}
to obtain, for all
\tau_{*}>0
,

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq 2^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\bigg(\frac{u_{n}(x_{0},\tau_{*})}{\tau_{*}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg).\end{split}

This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.10

(Smoothing effects)
.

Assume
q=p/(p-1)\in[1,\infty)
and
r\in(1,m]
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.8
and (

J
2

), we have that:

(a)

(
L^{r}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

For a.e.
\tau_{*}>0
,

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq\Big(\frac{q(m-1)^{\frac{2m-1}{m-1}}}{r-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{(m-1)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\tau_{*}^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\Big(\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}C_{J,p}^{\frac{m}{r}}\Big)^{\frac{q}{m}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\end{split}

where
C(m)=2^{\frac{m}{m-1}}
.

(b)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

For a.e.
\tau_{*}>0
,

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq(mqC(m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}})^{\frac{1}{m}}\tau_{*}^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}+\Big(\frac{mC(m)}{m-1}C_{J,p}\Big)^{q}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.\end{split}

Proof.

By Theorem
5.8
,

u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}u_{n}(x_{0},\tau_{*})+\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{m}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=:I+II.

(a) We use Lemma
5.4
(b)(ii) with
p=\infty
to get

II\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-r}\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{r}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

By the Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=\frac{m}{r}
and
\vartheta=m
, we estimate

II\leq\frac{m-r}{m}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m}+\frac{r}{m}\bigg(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{r}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{m}{r}}

and

I\leq\frac{1}{m}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})+\frac{m-1}{m}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}.

Collecting the terms yields

\begin{split}u_{n}^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})&\leq\frac{m}{m-1}\frac{m-r}{m}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}+\frac{m}{m-1}\frac{m-1}{m}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\frac{m}{m-1}\frac{r}{m}\bigg(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{r}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{m}{r}}\\
&=\frac{m-r}{m-1}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}+\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\frac{r}{m-1}\bigg(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{r}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{m}{r}}.\end{split}

Since

\frac{m-r}{m-1}=\frac{m-1+1-r}{m-1}=1-\frac{r-1}{m-1},

we can only absorb the
L^{\infty}
-norm on the left-hand side when
r>1
. Indeed,

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m}&\leq\frac{m-1}{r-1}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\frac{r}{r-1}\esssup_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\bigg(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{2\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}^{r}(x,t)J^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{m}{r}},\end{split}

and since
\|J^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|J\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
, we obtain by the Hölder inequality and Lemma
5.4
(b)(ii) with
p=q
that

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}&\leq\frac{m-1}{r-1}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}+\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}\|J\|_{L^{p}}^{\frac{m}{r}}\|u_{n}^{r}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{q}}^{\frac{m}{r}}\\
&\leq\frac{m-1}{r-1}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}+\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}\|J\|_{L^{p}}^{\frac{m}{r}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{m(q-1)}{q}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{m}{q}}.\end{split}

Apply the Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=q
to obtain

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}&\leq\frac{m-1}{r-1}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}+\frac{q-1}{q}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}\\
&\quad+\frac{r^{q}C(m)^{\frac{mq}{r}}}{q(r-1)^{q}}\|J\|_{L^{p}}^{\frac{mq}{r}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{r}}^{m},\end{split}

or,

(5.3)

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m}&\leq\frac{q(m-1)^{1+\frac{m}{m-1}}}{r-1}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\Big(\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}\|J\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{m}{r}}\Big)^{q}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m}.\end{split}

Finally, since
m>1
,
x\mapsto x^{\frac{1}{m}}
is concave and sub-additive on
[0,\infty)
, and we conclude.

(b) The Hölder inequality yields

\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{m}}^{m}\leq\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m-1}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}}.

Inserting this estimate into (
5.3
) with
r=m
, and then applying the Young inequalities with
\vartheta=m
give

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}&\leq\frac{q(m-1)^{1+\frac{m}{m-1}}}{m-1}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\frac{m-1}{m}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}+\frac{1}{m}\Big(\Big(\frac{mC(m)^{\frac{m}{m}}}{m-1}\|J\|_{L^{p}}^{\frac{m}{m}}\Big)^{q}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}}\Big)^{m},\end{split}

or

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{m}&\leq m\frac{q(m-1)^{1+\frac{m}{m-1}}}{m-1}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}+\Big(\Big(\frac{mC(m)}{m-1}\|J\|_{L^{p}}\Big)^{q}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{1}},\Big)^{m},\end{split}

which concludes the proof since
x\mapsto x^{\frac{1}{m}}
is sub-additive.
∎

The above results are not invariant under time-scaling (Lemma
4.3
). We thus rewrite them in a proper form:

Proposition 5.11

(Scaling-invariant smoothing effects)
.

Assume
q=p/(p-1)\in[1,\infty)
and
r\in(1,m]
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.8
and (

J
2

), we have that:

(a)

(
L^{r}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}2\Big(\frac{q(m-1)^{\frac{2m-1}{m-1}}}{r-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\Big(\frac{C(m)}{(m-1)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<t\leq t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\\
2\Big(\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}C_{J,p}^{\frac{m}{r}}\Big)^{\frac{q}{m}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $t>t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where

t_{0,n}:=C(m)^{\frac{r-q(m-1)}{r}}C_{J,p}^{-\frac{q(m-1)}{r}}\Big(\frac{q(r-1)^{q-1}(m-1)}{r^{q}}\Big)^{\frac{m-1}{m}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.

(b)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\begin{cases}2(mqC(m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}})^{\frac{1}{m}}t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<t\leq t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\\
2\Big(\frac{mC(m)}{m-1}C_{J,p}\Big)^{q}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $t>t_{0,n}$ a.e.,}\end{cases}

where

t_{0,n}:=(mq)^{\frac{m-1}{m}}\Big(\frac{m}{m-1}C_{J,p}\Big)^{-q(m-1)}C(m)^{1-q(m-1)}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{-(m-1)}.

Proof.

We only provide a proof for part (a) since (b) is similar.

Proposition
5.10
(a) gives

\begin{split}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}&\leq\Big(\frac{q(m-1)^{\frac{2m-1}{m-1}}C(m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}{r-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}+\Big(\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}C_{J,p}^{\frac{m}{r}}\Big)^{\frac{q}{m}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\tau_{*})\|_{L^{r}},\end{split}

but this result is not respecting the time-scaling (Lemma
4.3
):

\begin{split}\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\Lambda\tau_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}}&\leq\Big(\frac{q(m-1)^{\frac{2m-1}{m-1}}C(m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}{r-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\Lambda\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\\
&\quad+\Big(\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}C_{J,p}^{\frac{m}{r}}\Big)^{\frac{q}{m}}\Lambda^{\frac{1}{m-1}}\|u_{n}(\cdot,\Lambda\tau_{*})\|_{L^{r}}.\end{split}

By Lemma
5.4
(b)(ii) with
p=r
, we can optimize by requiring that

\begin{split}&\Big(\frac{q(m-1)^{\frac{2m-1}{m-1}}C(m)^{\frac{m}{m-1}}}{r-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)\Lambda\tau_{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}=\Big(\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}C_{J,p}^{\frac{m}{r}}\Big)^{\frac{q}{m}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{r}},\end{split}

or

\begin{split}\Lambda\tau_{*}=C(m)^{\frac{r-q(m-1)}{r}}C_{J,p}^{-\frac{q(m-1)}{r}}\Big(\frac{q(r-1)^{q-1}(m-1)}{r^{q}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{r}}^{m}}\Big)^{\frac{m-1}{m}}=:t_{0,n}.\end{split}

We obtain that

\|u_{n}(\cdot,t_{0,n})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2\Big(\frac{rC(m)^{\frac{m}{r}}}{r-1}C_{J,p}^{\frac{m}{r}}\Big)^{\frac{q}{m}}\|u_{0,n}\|_{L^{r}}.

To finish, we follow the proof of Proposition
4.18
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
5.1
follows as for Theorem
3.5
, except that we verify that the limit is a very weak solution in the sense of Definition
5.1
here.

6.
Smoothing effects VS Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev inequalities

In this section we investigate the connections between the validity of smoothing effects for solutions to diffusion equations and the validity of suitable functional inequalities of Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev (GNS) type, together with some limiting cases, and their dual counterparts, the Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev (HLS) type inequalities. As already mentioned, it is well-known since the celebrated work of Nash
[
97
]
that the ultracontractive estimate for solutions of the heat equation, i.e. (
GPME
) with
m=1
, are equivalent to a special GNS inequality. There has been an extensive literature on this nowadays classical topic, and theorems analogous to Theorem
6.1
below can be found in analysis textbooks, e.g.,
[
94
,
102
]
.

In the nonlinear setting much less is known, a first result in this direction has been given in
[
25
]
where, adapting the Gross method to the nonlinear setting, logarithmic Sobolev (LS) inequalities (of Euclidean type) implied
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing effects for porous medium-type equations (also on Riemannian manifolds). Indeed, LS inequalities are limiting cases of GNS inequalities, hence it is shown there how GNS inequalities imply smoothing effects. Later the equivalence between GNS and smoothing effects was established in
[
27
,
75
,
77
]
, see also
[
50
]
. In the nonlinear case, the Nash method does not work, and the classical alternative is provided by the celebrated Moser iteration, which was first introduced for linear parabolic equations
[
95
,
96
]
, then extended by various authors to the nonlinear setting, see
[
81
,
54
,
86
,
32
,
78
,
98
,
29
,
84
,
21
,
85
,
92
]
. Another classical possibility is the DeGiorgi method, which can be adapted to the nonlinear setting. It also shows how functional inequalities imply regularity properties of solutions, see for instance
[
68
,
69
]
. Once GNS imply smoothing, it is often possible to prove the converse implication, establishing equivalence, cf. Theorem
6.11
.

In the pioneering paper
[
7
]
, see also
[
102
]
, the equivalences of different Sobolev, GNS, Nash, LS, and Poincaré inequalities are established. We recall some of the precise results in Lemma
6.15
. Roughly speaking, the idea is that all functional inequalities that can be true with a suitable quadratic form are equivalent: We will analyze mainly two classes that we call Sobolev or Poincaré, since they are equivalent respectively to
L^{q}
–
L^{\infty}
- or to
L^{q}
–
L^{p}
-smoothing effects for the associated linear equation, i.e., (
GPME
) with
m=1
. We add other equivalences and implications related to (
GPME
) with
m>1
, which is the main purpose of this section, see Figure
5
below. Let us also mention that a more direct proof of the equivalence between Nash and LS can be obtained by the methods of
[
36
]
combined with the 4-norm inequality of
[
26
]
.

We want to emphasize that sometimes the nonlinear diffusion enjoys smoothing while the linear counterpart does not. The nonlinear smoothing must then be equivalent to a functional inequality that has to be weaker than any GNS (or any other functional inequality equivalent to Sobolev), otherwise it would imply smoothing in the linear case. We provided explicit examples of this phenomenon in Section
5
. This allowed to conclude that while linear smoothing implies nonlinear smoothing, the viceversa is not true in general, see Theorem
3.9
and Remark
3.10
. The crucial ingredient to prove the smoothing for the nonlinear (when the linear does not smooth) is the Green function method, developed in the previous sections.

So far, the panorama of implications does not include Green functions, only heat kernels. It can, however, be shown using Legendre transform that Sobolev and HLS are equivalent, see Lemma
6.7
. Dual norms indeed involve Green functions, and an upper bound on the Green function implies the HLS, hence a Sobolev inequality. The Green function method thus replaces the use of Sobolev inequalities and iterations à la Moser or à la DeGiorgi with simpler integral estimates, and provides a solid alternative to those methods. Moreover, having at disposal estimates on the Green function seems to be more versatile in the sense that the method surprisingly works when the linear counterpart does not smooth. The latter must indicate that the Green function estimate cannot always provide strong functional inequalities which would imply smoothing in the linear case.

In many examples, the Green function estimates necessary for the method to work, are derived from heat kernel bounds, or via Fourier transform, see Section
7
. As we shall explain below, in the nonlocal case, GNS is not sufficient to prove smoothing effects via Moser iterations. One also needs the Stroock-Varopoulos inequality
[
107
,
111
]
, which somehow replaces the Sobolev chain rule in the local case.

One of the merits of this paper is represented from the fact that having Green function estimates—and then also boundedness estimates—allow to prove GNS (with the quadratic form already adjusted to the operator) that then have many other applications. In the nonlocal case, proving GNS is not an easy task for general quadratic forms, see
[
70
,
10
,
43
]
. We provide here a PDE proof of many functional inequalities that can have their own interest. We also prove the validity of some weak GNS, as a consequence of the nonlinear smoothing. See Section
7
for a rich list of examples of operators included in our theory.

Optimal or explicit constants in fractional GNS type inequalities is mostly an unexplored topic. In the local case, the sharp classical Nash inequality has been proven in
[
42
,
36
]
by different methods. Sharp GNS have been proven in
[
61
]
by entropy methods and nonlinear flows, and by mass transportation techniques in
[
48
]
. Quantitative and constructive stability for GNS has recently been proven in
[
21
]
, to which we refer the reader for thorough historical and bibliographical information, also on Sobolev and related inequalities. We refrain from a thorough discussion here. As for functional inequalities related to nonlocal operators or fractional Sobolev spaces, to the best of our knowledge only a few contributions are present in literature: optimal fractional Sobolev inequalities are discussed in
[
49
]
, while optimal fractional GNS in
[
10
]
. Fractional Hardy inequalities are studied in
[
70
]
. Improved Sobolev have been studied in
[
99
]
by means of concentration compactness methods. We apologize in advance, in case we are missing important contributions in these directions, but in this paper we do not address the question of optimal inequalities, we just establish their validity with a (computable) constant.

Throughout this section,
C>0
is a constant (that might change) which depends on
N
,
\alpha
,
m
, and the underlying Green function, but not on any norm of
u
or
u_{0}
. We will use the notation

\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f,g]:=\int f(-\mathfrak{L})[g]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]:=\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f,f],

and we will write, for
q>0
,

\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|f(x)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}=\|f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

even though it is not a proper norm when
q\in(0,1)
.

6.1.
The well-known linear case (
m=1
)

We state and prove the following form of the Nash-type theorem
[
97
]
, adapted to our setting.

Theorem 6.1

(Linear equivalences)
.

Assume
\alpha\in(0,2]
and
2^{*}=2N/(N-\alpha)
. The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
Let
u
be a solution of (
GPME
) with
m=1
and initial data
u_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(b)

(Sobolev)
For all
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap{\rm dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}})
,

\|f\|_{L^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(c)

(Nash)
For all
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap{\rm dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}})
,

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\vartheta:=\frac{1}{2}\frac{2^{*}-2}{2^{*}-1}.

(d)

(On-diagonal heat kernel bounds)
The heat kernel
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
satisfies

0\leq\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}.

Remark 6.2
.

The case
\alpha=2
is well-known, and we refer the reader to e.g. Lemma 2.1.2 and Theorem 2.4.6 in
[
55
]
(see also
[
94
]
). In the context of Lévy operators
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with an absolutely continuous measure
\mu
, it is worth mentioning that as long as

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\gtrsim\frac{1}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}

in (
1
), we are in the case
\alpha\in(0,2)
, cf.
[
74
, Proposition 2.6]
. One can also replace
N/\alpha
by
\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\alpha_{i})^{-1}
as in the Sobolev inequality corresponding to the sum of onedimensional fractional Laplacians
[
43
, Theorem 2.4]
. Examples of (some of the above) equivalences in the nonpower case can be found in e.g. Proposition 3 and Lemma 5 in
[
90
]
. We also refer to
[
37
]
which explores various equivalences between Nash inequalities and
L^{q}
–
L^{p}
-smoothing estimates for Lévy operators (see also
[
41
]
).

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\\
\lesssim|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}\end{array}
Sobolev inequality
L^{1}\text{--}L^{\infty}\text{-smoothing}
Nash inequality
\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{On-diagonal}\\
\text{heat kernel bounds}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{Off-diagonal}\\
\text{heat kernel bounds}\end{array}
Via HLS
L^{p}\text{-interp.}
Via HLS
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(\cdot,0)=\delta_{x_{0}}
Energy est.
\text{Dual }L^{\infty}
\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{Representation}\\
\text{formula}\end{array}
Definition
Integration

Figure 4.
Implications in the linear case. Note that off-diagonal heat kernel bounds provide the strongest information unless we know how to deduce those bounds from the on-diagonal ones (like in

and

). In the latter case, any piece of information is equivalent.

The proof is divided into several independent results. By interpolation in
L^{p}
, we immediately have:

Lemma 6.3

(Sobolev implies Nash)
.

Assume
\alpha\in(0,2]
and
2^{*}=2N/(N-\alpha)
. If

\|f\|_{L^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}},

then

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\vartheta:=\frac{1}{2}\frac{2^{*}-2}{2^{*}-1}.

Lemma 6.4

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing VS Nash inequality)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.1
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(b)

(Nash)

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\vartheta:=\frac{1}{2}\frac{2^{*}-2}{2^{*}-1}.

Proof.

Follows by Theorem 8.16 in
[
94
]
7
7

7

We warn the reader about a small typo in the remark after Theorem 8.16 in
[
94
]
:
(f,Lf)=\int fLf
is indeed
\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
when
L=-\Delta
.
(see also Section 4.1 in
[
102
]
). There, the Nash inequality is equivalent with an
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing effect. An intermediate step is the
L^{1}
–
L^{2}
-smoothing effect, which can be extended to
L^{\infty}
by the Nash duality trick:

\begin{split}\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}&=\sup_{\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}=1}\bigg|\int u(t)\phi\bigg|=\sup_{\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}=1}\bigg|\int S_{t}[u_{0}]\phi\bigg|=\sup_{\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}=1}\bigg|\int S_{\frac{t}{2}}[S_{\frac{t}{2}}[u_{0}]]\phi\bigg|\\
&=\sup_{\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}=1}\bigg|\int S_{\frac{t}{2}}[u_{0}]S_{\frac{t}{2}}[\phi]\bigg|\leq\sup_{\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}=1}\|S_{\frac{t}{2}}[u_{0}]\|_{L^{2}}\|S_{\frac{t}{2}}[\phi]\|_{L^{2}}.\\
\end{split}

Here we used that the semigroup
S_{t}
is self-adjoint, and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

To obtain the
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing in the nonlinear case (
m>1
), the Nash inequality is usually replaced by the Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev inequality:

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)},

where

2\leq p<2^{*},\qquad 1\leq q<p,\qquad\vartheta:=\frac{q}{p}\frac{2^{*}-p}{2^{*}-q}.

Then the Moser iteration can be used to obtain the desired result. In the next section, we show that we indeed need less than the above inequality to perform all the necessary steps.

Lemma 6.6

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing and heat kernel bounds)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.1
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(b)

(On-diagonal heat kernel bounds)

0\leq\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}

Proof.

(a)
\Longrightarrow
(b). We apply Theorem 2.6.20 in
[
82
]
. Formally,
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
solves (
GPME
) with
m=1
and
\delta_{x_{0}}
as initial data. Hence, by an approximation argument and the lower semicontinuity of the
L^{\infty}
-norm, we arrive at part (b).

(b)
\Longrightarrow
(a). Since
C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
-initial data produce solutions that satisfy the representation formula
u(x,t)=\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(\cdot,t)\ast u_{0}(x)
and

|u(x,t)|\leq\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(\cdot,t)\ast|u_{0}|(x)\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

we can again do an approximation argument to show (a).
∎

It remains to prove that the Nash inequality implies the Sobolev inequality. For
C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}
-functions, such a result can be found in
[
7
]
. However, for semigroups in
L^{2}
, we will consider an indirect path through the inverse of the square root of the operator.

Legendre duality allows to establish equivalences between Sobolev and Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev (HLS) inequalities:

Lemma 6.7

(Sobolev VS HLS)
.

Assume
\alpha\in(0,2]
,
2^{*}=2N/(N-\alpha)
, and
(2^{*})^{\prime}:=2^{*}/(2^{*}-1)=2N/(N+\alpha)
. The following inequalities are equivalent:

(a)

(Sobolev)
For all
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap{\rm dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}})
,

\|f\|_{L^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{\frac{1}{2}}[f]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(b)

(HLS)
For all
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap{\rm dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}})
,

\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{-\frac{1}{2}}[g]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{(2^{*})^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

The proof is based on the Legendre transform, see e.g. Proposition 7.4 in
[
30
]
, that we learned by Lieb
[
93
]
. Lemma
6.4
already established that the Nash inequality implies the
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing. The next lemma then finishes the proof of Theorem

.

Lemma 6.8

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing VS HLS)
.

Assume
\alpha\in(0,2]
and
(2^{*})^{\prime}:=2^{*}/(2^{*}-1)=2N/(N+\alpha)
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

(

–

Let

) with

and initial data

, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(b)

(HLS)
For all
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap{\rm dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}})
,

\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{-\frac{1}{2}}[g]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{(2^{*})^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Proof.

(a)
\Longrightarrow
(b). We apply Theorem II.2.7 in

]
with

and

.

(b)

.

Finally, we relate Green function estimates with all of the above equivalences.

Lemma 6.9

(Green VS HLS)
.

Assume (
2
),
\alpha\in(0,2]
, and
(2^{*})^{\prime}=2^{*}/(2^{*}-1)=2N/(N+\alpha)
. If

0\leq\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq C|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)},

then, for all
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap{\rm dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}})
,

\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{-\frac{1}{2}}[g]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{(2^{*})^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Remark 6.10
.

The above assumption on the Green function is stronger than (
2
). We refer the reader to
[
88
]
for a discussion on the validity of such an upper bound.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

This is essentially Theorem 7.5 in
[
30
]
, which we restate here for completeness.

A direct calculation gives

\begin{split}&\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{-\frac{1}{2}}[f]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(-\mathfrak{L})^{-\frac{1}{2}}[f](-\mathfrak{L})^{-\frac{1}{2}}[f]\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}[f]\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(x)\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x}(y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(x)\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|x-y|^{-(N-\alpha)}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=C\|(-\Delta)^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}}[f]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2}.\end{split}

The classical Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev

\|(-\Delta)^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}}[f]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{(2^{*})^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

then provides the result.
∎

6.2.
The nonlinear case (
m>1
)

While in the linear case, the Nash method works perfectly, in the nonlinear case, the Nash method simply does not work since the “nonlinear heat semigroup” is not symmetric. On the other hand, the Moser iteration, which provides an alternative proof in the linear case, can be adapted to work also in the nonlinear case, and it shows how to prove smoothing effects from GNS inequalities also in the nonlinear setting. However GNS are not sufficient to perform Moser iteration in the nonlocal setting, another ingredient is needed: the so-called Stroock-Varopoulos inequalities. Let us briefly explain how this works.

3.5

Figure 5.
Implications in the nonlinear case. Note that still the off-diagonal heat kernel bounds provide the strongest piece of information. However, we also see that because of (
2
), on-diagonal heat kernel bounds ensure a closed loop in the nonlinear case, assuming that
[
7
]
applies.

Assume that there exists
2^{*}\geq 2
such that the Sobolev-Poincaré type inequality holds (
2^{*}=2
being the Poincaré case)

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(-\mathfrak{L})f\,\mathrm{d}x=C\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]=C\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{\frac{1}{2}}[u]\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

where the last equality is true whenever the operator
-\mathfrak{L}
has an extension to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. By simple interpolation of
L^{p}
-norms, for
\tilde{p}\in[(1+m)/m,2)
and
\tilde{q}\in[1/m,\tilde{p})
,

\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{q}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\quad\text{where}\quad\vartheta:=\vartheta(2^{*}).

When dealing with energy estimates in the local case, a calculus equality allows us to do the Moser iteration:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u^{p-1}(-\Delta)[u^{m}]\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\nabla u^{p-1}\cdot\nabla u^{m}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{4m(p-1)}{(p+m-1)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big|\nabla u^{\frac{p+m-1}{2}}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

However, we just need an inequality, which in the nonlocal case has been proven by Stroock and Varopoulos
[
107
,
111
]
(cf.
[
37
, Proposition 4.11]
or
[
64
, Lemma 4.10]
): For the same constant as above,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u^{p-1}(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]\,\mathrm{d}x\gtrsim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u^{\frac{p+m-1}{2}}(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{\frac{p+m-1}{2}}]\,\mathrm{d}x\eqsim\big\|(-\mathfrak{L})^{\frac{1}{2}}[u^{\frac{p+m-1}{2}}]\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Combining the two above inequalities, one gets

(M)

\int u^{p-1}(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]\geq\frac{4m(p-1)}{(p+m-1)^{2}}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[u^{\frac{p+m-1}{2}}]\geq\frac{4m(p-1)}{C^{2}(p+m-1)^{2}}\frac{\|u\|_{L^{\tilde{p}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}^{\frac{2}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}{\|u\|_{L^{\tilde{q}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}^{\frac{2\vartheta}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}.

The above condition is the key to prove the following:

Theorem 6.11

(Green functions satisfying (
2
))
.

Assume (

H
m

). Then
the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

(
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)
Let
u
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Ct^{-N\theta_{1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{1}},

where
\theta_{1}=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
.

(ii)

(Subcritical GNS)
For
\tilde{p}\in[(1+m)/m,2)
and
\tilde{q}\in[1/m,\tilde{p})
, and for all
f\in L^{\tilde{q}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap{\rm dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}})
we have

\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{q}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\quad\text{where}\quad\vartheta:=\frac{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{p}}\frac{2^{*}-\tilde{p}}{2^{*}-\tilde{q}}.

When we have at our disposal Green functions satisfying (
2
) then, by Theorem
3.1
(a), we have the above nonlinear smoothing effect. This turns out to be equivalent to a family of subcritical Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev, which then are equivalent to Sobolev, see Lemma
6.15
below. In order to show that subcritical GNS imply nonlinear smoothing, we will perform a Moser iteration. We refer to
[
28
, Section 3]
for a more detailed exposition of the Moser iteration in the fast diffusion case
0<m<1
, in the context of bounded domains. It also contains a detailed discussion about the Green function method versus the Moser iteration.

When we have integrable Green functions (
2
), we can obtain absolute bounds (i.e. independent of the initial datum), as in the case of bounded domains
[
34
,
35
,
24
]
. Such bounds imply weak GNS inequalities, which are equivalent to Poincaré inequalities (Lemma
6.15
). We notice that it is not possible (to the best of our knowledge) to prove the converse implication via the Moser iteration. In fact, the constant simply blows up at the limit
p\to\infty
. A similar discussion can be found in
[
75
,
77
]
. However we have seen in Theorem
3.7
a simple proof of the absolute bounds with the Green function method, so that we can conclude that integrable Green functions imply Poincaré-type inequalities as follows:

Proposition 6.12

(Green functions satisfying (
2
))
.

Assume (

H
m

). Given the following statements:

(Absolute bound)

u

) with initial data

(ii)

(Subcritical GNS)
For
\tilde{p}\in[(1+m)/m,2)
and
\tilde{q}\in[1/m,\tilde{p})
, and for all

we have

\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{q}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\quad\text{where}\quad\vartheta:=\frac{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{p}}\frac{2-\tilde{p}}{2-\tilde{q}}.

Then (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii).

In order to prove the above theorem and proposition, we need a few results that we prefer to state and prove separately since they have their own interest. We shall start with the fact that (any) GNS is equivalent to some
L^{q}
–

-smoothing. This can be directly seen by the Stroock-Varopoulos inequality.

Proposition 6.13

(
L^{q}
–

).

(Green functions satisfying (

Remark 6.14
.

(a)

Let us make some comments on part (a). First of all, note that
\vartheta
is nothing but the standard quantity appearing in interpolation between
L^{p}
-norms with
\tilde{q}<\tilde{p}<2^{*}
. We also see that when formally
m\to 1^{-}
,
\tilde{p}=2
and
\tilde{q}=1
in the above subcritical GNS inequality, and we recover the critical Nash inequality (see Section
6.1
). In our case
m>1
, and that is why we call it subcritical. Note, however, that the standard GNS inequality (see Remark
6.5
) is not included as a special case here.

(b)

The proof reveals that GNS inequalities always imply
L^{q}
–
L^{p}
-smoothing effects, but the opposite implication requires further assumptions. Actually, the equivalence which is always true is the one between
L^{1}
–
L^{m+1}
-smoothing effects and subcritical Nash inequalities. In fact, even operators only yielding boundedness estimates in the form of Theorem
3.5
(see also Theorem
5.1
), still enjoy the latter equivalence.

To provide a proof, we need:

Lemma 6.15

(
[
7
,
102
]
)
.

Assume (

H
m

) and
f\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Then:

(a)

(Sobolev)
The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

(Sobolev)

\|f\|_{L^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(ii)

(Subcritical GNS)
For
\tilde{p}\in[(1+m)/m,2)
and
\tilde{q}\in[1/m,\tilde{p})
,

\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{q}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\quad\text{where}\quad\vartheta:=\frac{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{p}}\frac{2^{*}-\tilde{p}}{2^{*}-\tilde{q}}.

(b)

(Poincaré)
The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

(Poincaré)

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(ii)

(Subcritical GNS)
For
\tilde{p}\in[(1+m)/m,2)
and
\tilde{q}\in[1/m,\tilde{p})
,

\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\tilde{q}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\vartheta}\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\vartheta)}\quad\text{where}\quad\vartheta:=\frac{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{p}}\frac{2-\tilde{p}}{2-\tilde{q}}.

Remark 6.16
.

(a)

In both cases, we simply check that
q
in
[
7
]
is respectively given by
2^{*}
and
2
. It is also worth noting, that “any” family of Sobolev/Poincaré-type inequalities is equivalent with the Sobolev/Poincaré inequality. As a consequence, subcritical GNS inequalities are equivalent with subcritical Nash inequalities, and then also equivalent with the standard Nash and GNS inequalities, respectively. However, the subcritical ones might be easier to prove in the nonlinear setting.

(b)

The case
q=2
is kind of curious since it yields a Poincaré inequality in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Such an inequality is not fulfilled in e.g. the case
\mathfrak{L}=\Delta
as the spectrum is nonnegative. Hence, it provides the intuition that absolute bounds holds if the spectrum of the operator is positive (e.g. as in the case
-\mathfrak{L}=(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
).

Proof of Proposition
6.13
.

(a) (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). By the particular choices
1=q<p=m+1
, we immediately have the corresponding
L^{1}
–
L^{m+1}
-smoothing effect. Then, direct and formal
8
8

8

We have decided to present the differential version of these estimates because the main idea is easier to follow. This is rigorous for strong solutions, for instance when
\partial_{t}u\in L^{1}
. It often happens that bounded weak solutions are strong, possibly under some additional assumptions on
\mathfrak{L}
, see
[
59
]
. These formal computations can be justified rigorously in several different ways. One possibility is to use Steklov averages and Grönwall-type inequalities. It is beyond the scope of this paper to justify these computations, but we remark that they can be shown to hold for the mild solutions constructed in Appendix
E
, through standard approximations. The energy computations, namely the ones involving the
L^{m+1}
norm, are always true for weak energy solutions (of which mild solutions are a subclass of).
computations show that

(6.1)

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int u^{m+1}=\int\partial_{t}(u^{m+1})=(m+1)\int u^{m}\partial_{t}u=-(m+1)\int u^{m}(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}],

and also, since
\mathfrak{L}
is symmetric and
u
solves (
GPME
),

(6.2)

\begin{split}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int u^{m}(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]&=2\int\partial_{t}(u^{m})(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]=2m\int u^{m-1}\partial_{t}u(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]\\
&=-2m\int u^{m-1}\Big((-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]\Big)^{2}\leq 0.\end{split}

Hence, by (
6.2
), we obtain in (
6.1
) that

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int u^{m+1}\geq-(m+1)\int u_{0}^{m}(-\mathfrak{L})[u_{0}^{m}]=-(m+1)\mathcal{Q}_{-\mathfrak{L}}[u_{0}^{m}].

We then integrate over
(0,T)
and use
L^{1}
–
L^{m+1}
-smoothing effect to get

\begin{split}-(m+1)Q_{-\mathfrak{L}}[u_{0}^{m}]T&\leq\|u(T)\|_{L^{m+1}}^{m+1}-\|u_{0}\|_{L^{m+1}}^{m+1}\leq CT^{-N((m+1)-1)\theta_{1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{m+1}}}-\|u_{0}\|_{L^{m+1}}^{m+1},\end{split}

or, by taking
f:=u_{0}^{m}
,

\|f\|_{L^{\frac{m+1}{m}}}^{\frac{m+1}{m}}\leq F(T):=C\|f\|_{L^{\frac{1}{m}}}^{\frac{1}{m}\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{m+1}}}T^{-N((m+1)-1)\theta_{1}}+(m+1)Q_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]T.

The inequality is still valid if we infimize
F
over
T>0
, and this gives the subcritical Nash inequality, i.e.,
\tilde{p}=(1+m)/m
and
\tilde{q}=1/m
in the stated subcritical GNS. Since the subcritical Nash inequality is a subfamily of the subcritical GNS, it is equivalent with the Sobolev inequality and then equivalent with GNS by Lemma
6.15
.

(a) (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i). Note that (ii) with
f=u^{\frac{p+m-1}{2}}
and the Stroock-Varopoulos inequality gives (
M
). Direct and formal
9
9

9

Again, it is beyond the scope of this paper to justify these computations, but they hold for e.g. the mild solutions constructed in Appendix
E
under some possibly additional assumptions on
\mathfrak{L}
. See also the previous footnote.
calculations and the
L^{p}
-decay (Proposition
4.1
(b)(ii)) give

(6.3)

\begin{split}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int u^{p}&=\int\partial_{t}(u^{p})=p\int u^{p-1}\partial_{t}u=-p\int u^{p-1}(-\mathfrak{L})[u^{m}]\\
&\leq-\frac{4mp(p-1)}{C^{2}(p+m-1)^{2}}\frac{\|u\|_{L^{\tilde{p}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}^{\frac{2}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}{\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\tilde{q}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}^{\frac{2\vartheta}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}}}=-\frac{4mp(p-1)}{C^{2}(p+m-1)^{2}}\frac{\|u\|_{L^{p}}^{1+\sigma}}{\|u_{0}\|_{L^{q}}^{\frac{2(2^{*}-\tilde{p})}{2^{*}(\tilde{p}-\tilde{q})}q}}\end{split}

Where we have chosen:

\tilde{p}:=\frac{2p}{p+m-1},\qquad\tilde{q}:=\frac{2q}{p+m-1}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\sigma:=\frac{2^{*}(2-\tilde{p})+\tilde{q}(2^{*}-2)}{2^{*}(\tilde{p}-\tilde{q})},

and this choice is consistent with our assumptions. We also have that

\frac{2}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}=\frac{2(2^{*}-\tilde{q})}{2^{*}(\tilde{p}-\tilde{q})}p\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{2\vartheta}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}=\frac{2(2^{*}-\tilde{p})}{2^{*}(\tilde{p}-\tilde{q})}q.

so that, integrating the differential inequality we get (i). The proof of part (a) is concluded.

(b) (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Follows by a similar argument as in (a) (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii), except that

F(T):=C\|f\|_{L^{\frac{1}{m}}}^{\frac{1}{m}}T^{-\frac{m}{m-1}}+(m+1)Q_{-\mathfrak{L}}[f]T.

(b) (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i). We argue exactly as in (a) (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i), but now

\frac{2}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}=\frac{2-\tilde{q}}{\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}}p,\qquad\frac{2\vartheta}{1-\vartheta}\frac{p+m-1}{2}=\frac{2-\tilde{p}}{\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}}q,

\frac{1}{\sigma}=\frac{p-q}{m-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{2-\tilde{p}}{\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}}\frac{1}{\sigma}=1.

This yields the desired estimate.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.11
.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i). In what follows, we will just sketch the essential parts of the proof, in order to focus on the main ideas. The proof can moreover be made rigorous by standard approximation techniques. Let us first remark that it is enough to prove the following
L^{m+1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing effect:

(6.4)

\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Ct^{-N\theta_{m+1}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{m+1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha(m+1)\theta_{m+1}}.

Indeed, the claimed
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing effects is then deduced by applying Lemma
B.2
of Appendix
B
.

In order to prove the smoothing effect (
6.4
), we will iterate “Moser style” the
L^{p}
–
L^{q}
-smoothing effects Proposition
6.13
(a)(i): Let us define
p_{0}=m+1
and
p_{k}=2^{k}p_{0}
for each
k\geq 1
, and
t_{k}
such that
t_{k}-t_{k-1}=\frac{t-t_{0}}{2^{k}}
, so that inequality Proposition
6.13
(a)(i) becomes

\displaystyle\|u(t_{k})\|_{L^{p_{k}}}\leq I_{k}^{\frac{N(p_{k}-p_{k-1})}{p_{k}}\theta_{k-1}}\|u(t_{k-1})\|_{L^{p_{k-1}}}^{\frac{p_{k-1}\,\theta_{k-1}}{p_{k}\,\theta_{k}}}\qquad\mbox{with}\qquad I_{k}\eqsim\frac{p_{k}}{t_{k}-t_{k-1}}\eqsim 4^{k}

where
\theta_{k}:=\theta_{p_{k}}=(\alpha p_{k}+N(m-1))^{-1}
. More precisely, we have that we can estimate
I_{k}
uniformly as follows:

I_{k}:=C\frac{(p_{k}+m-1)^{2}}{4m(m-1)(p_{k}-1)}\frac{1}{t_{k}-t_{k-1}}\leq 4^{k}\frac{C}{t-t_{0}},

for some constant
C>0
that depends only on
m,N
.

Then we iterate

\displaystyle\|u(t_{k})\|_{L^{p_{k}}}

\displaystyle\leq I_{k}^{\frac{N(p_{k}-p_{k-1})}{p_{k}}\theta_{k-1}}\|u(t_{k-1})\|_{L^{p_{k-1}}}^{\frac{p_{k-1}\,\theta_{k-1}}{p_{k}\,\theta_{k}}}

\displaystyle\leq I_{k}^{\frac{N(p_{k}-p_{k-1})}{p_{k}}\theta_{k-1}}I_{k-1}^{\frac{N(p_{k-1}-p_{k-2})}{\cancel{p_{k-1}}}\theta_{k-2}\frac{\cancel{p_{k-1}}\theta_{k-1}}{p_{k}\theta_{k}}}\|u(t_{k-2})\|_{L^{p_{k-2}}}^{\frac{p_{k-2}\theta_{k-2}}{\cancel{p_{k-1}}\cancel{\theta_{k-1}}}\frac{\cancel{p_{k-1}}\,\cancel{\theta_{k-1}}}{p_{k}\,\theta_{k}}}

\displaystyle\;\;\vdots

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}I_{j}^{\frac{N(p_{j}-p_{j-1})}{p_{k}}\frac{\theta_{j}\theta_{j-1}}{\theta_{k}}}\;\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{p_{0}}}^{\frac{p_{0}\,\theta_{p_{0}}}{p_{k}\theta_{k}}}\leq\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(4^{j}\frac{\overline{c}}{t-t_{0}}\bigg)^{\frac{N(\theta_{j-1}-\theta_{j})}{\alpha}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{k}\theta_{k}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{p_{0}}}^{\frac{p_{0}\,\theta_{p_{0}}}{p_{k}\theta_{k}}}.

Finally, letting
k\to\infty
, it is easy to see that

\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(4^{j}\frac{C}{t-t_{0}}\bigg)^{\frac{N(\theta_{j-1}-\theta_{j})}{\alpha}}\leq 2^{\frac{N}{\alpha^{2}p_{0}\,p_{k}\theta_{k}}}\left(\frac{C}{t-t_{0}}\right)^{\frac{N(\theta_{p_{0}}-\theta_{k})}{\alpha}}

so that, using the lower semicontinuity of the
L^{\infty}
norm, we get

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\|u(t_{k})\|_{L^{p_{k}}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(2^{\frac{N}{\alpha^{2}{p_{0}}\,p_{k}\theta_{k}}}\left(\frac{C}{t-t_{0}}\right)^{\frac{N(\theta_{p_{0}}-\theta_{k})}{\alpha}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{k}\theta_{k}}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{p_{0}}}^{\frac{p_{0}\,\theta_{p_{0}}}{p_{k}\theta_{k}}}\leq C\frac{\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{p_{0}}}^{\alpha p_{0}\,\theta_{p_{0}}}}{(t-t_{0})^{N\theta_{p_{0}}}}\,.

This proves the desired inequality (
6.4
) and concludes the proof of (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i).

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Follows by Theorem
B.1
of Appendix
B
, which states that
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
imply
L^{p}
–
L^{q}
-smoothing effects, which in turn imply subcritical GNS, by Proposition
6.13
(a). This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.17
.

This proof holds for all
m\geq 1
, so in particular it also shows that subcritical GNS imply smoothing also in the linear case
m=1
, providing an alternative proof of the implication (b)
\Longrightarrow
(a) or (c)
\Longrightarrow
(a) in Theorem
6.1
. When
m\in(0,1)
, which corresponds to the fast diffusion case, the same proof works as well, but we need to require further integrability on the initial datum in order to perform the iteration, as thoroughly explained in e.g.
[
28
, Section 3]
(and also
[
54
,
32
,
29
]
for the local case).

Finally, we have:

Proof of Proposition
6.12
.

By Lemma
B.2
(with
\gamma=0
) of Appendix
B
, item (i) implies Proposition
6.13
(b)(i), and hence, item (ii) holds.
∎

7.
Various examples

This section is devoted to study the operators whose Green functions satisfy assumptions (
2
)–(
2
), and hence, which smoothing effects are satisfied by such operators. As a consequence of Proposition
D.3
, we have:

Proposition 7.1
.

Assume that the operator
-\mathfrak{L}
is linear, symmetric, nonnegative, and moreover, densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Then (
2
) holds, and the Green functions of
-\mathfrak{L}
and
I-\mathfrak{L}
are respectively given by

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t

and

\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
is the corresponding heat kernel of
-\mathfrak{L}
.

Let us illustrate these formulas through Fourier analysis. Denote by
\sigma_{-\mathfrak{L}}
the Fourier symbol of the operator
-\mathfrak{L}
. Then the heat kernel can be expressed as

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[\textup{e}^{-\sigma_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\cdot)t}\big](x-x_{0})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textup{e}^{-\sigma_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\xi)t}\textup{e}^{2\pi\textup{i}(x-x_{0})\cdot\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\xi,

and

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-\sigma_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\xi)t}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)\textup{e}^{2\pi\textup{i}(x-x_{0})\cdot\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{1}{\sigma_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\xi)}\textup{e}^{2\pi\textup{i}(x-x_{0})\cdot\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\xi=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\frac{1}{\sigma_{-\mathfrak{L}}(\xi)}\Big](x-x_{0}).\end{split}

We also refer to the well-written book
[
17
]
, which provides many examples of Green functions and a good introduction to potential theory.

In the examples that follows, we will need

(7.1)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-tr}r^{\vartheta}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{\Gamma(\vartheta+1)}{t^{\vartheta+1}}<\infty\qquad\text{whenever $\vartheta>-1$},

where
\Gamma
is the gamma function.

7.1.
On the assumption (
2
)

As demonstrated in Theorem
3.1
, assumption (
2
) leads to the estimate

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-N\theta_{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$}

for weak dual solutions of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. Let us provide some concrete examples of operators
-\mathfrak{L}
in (
GPME
) whose Green functions satisfy (
2
).

Lemma 7.2
.

The fractional Laplacian/Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
with
\alpha\in(0,2]
has a Green function which satisfies (
2
).

Remark 7.3
.

Let us mention that heat kernel estimates for the Laplacian and the fractional Laplacian dates back to Fourier
[
73
, Chapter IX Section II]
(see also
[
71
, Section 2.3]
) and Blumenthal and Getoor
[
16
]
, respectively.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

Assume
\alpha\in(0,2)
. By Lemma 2 in Chapter V.1 in
[
106
]
,

\mathbb{G}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x)=\mathscr{F}^{-1}\big[|\cdot|^{-\alpha}\big](x-x_{0})\eqsim|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}.

Now,

\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\eqsim\int_{0}^{R}r^{-(N-\alpha)}r^{N-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\eqsim R^{\alpha}.

Moreover, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})
,

\mathbb{G}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x)\lesssim R^{-(N-\alpha)},

and the result follows.

Assume
\alpha=2
. The result is classical and can e.g. be found in
[
55
, Section 1.1.8]
. We get that
\mathbb{G}_{-\Delta}^{x_{0}}(x)\eqsim|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-2)}
which satisfies (
2
) with
\alpha=2
.
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

Any operator
\mathfrak{L}
whose Green function satisfies

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\lesssim|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}\qquad\text{for some $\alpha\in(0,2]$}

will fulfil (
2
).

Remark 7.5
.

By Lemma
6.9
, the above assumption on the Green function implies that the corresponding operator satisfies the Sobolev inequality. Again, we also refer to
[
88
]
for a further discussion.

Lemma 7.6
.

Assume that the real matrix
[a_{ij}]_{i,j=1,\ldots,N}
is nonnegative and symmetric and
\mathfrak{L}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}a_{ij}\partial_{x_{i}x_{j}}^{2}
. Given the following statements:

(i)

There exist constants
C,c>0
such that

c|y|^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}a_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}\leq C|y|^{2}.

(ii)

There exist constants
C,c>0
such that

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq ct^{-\frac{N}{2}}\textup{exp}\Big(-C\frac{|x-x_{0}|^{2}}{t}\Big).

(iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq C|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-2)}.

We have (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii).

Remark 7.7
.

(a)

The heat kernel bound is (up to constants) the same as for the regular Laplacian. This is not surprising in the constant coefficient case since the operator is, up to a translation, the Laplacian. The more interesting case is of course when the coefficients are
(x,t)
-dependent, see also
[
83
, Section 2.9]
and the classical
[
3
]
. For a similar result in the fractional setting, we refer to
[
89
]
.

(b)

The upper bound in statement (i) might seem superfluous, but the constant inside the exponential function in (ii) depends on it.

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Follows by
[
55
, Corollary 3.2.8]
(see also
[
3
]
).

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii). Follows by
[
55
, Theorem 3.1.1]
(see also
[
3
]
).
∎

The nonlocal counterpart is somehow when the Lévy measure is comparable to the measure of the fractional Laplacian.

Lemma 7.8
.

Assume
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
and (
1
). Given the following statements, for
\alpha\in(0,2)
:

(i)

There exist constants
C,c>0
such that

\frac{c}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\leq\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\leq\frac{C}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C\min\big\{t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}},t|x-x_{0}|^{-(N+\alpha)}\big\}.

(iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq C|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}.

We have (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii).

Remark 7.9
.

We can also slightly weaken the assumption on the lower bound: There exist constants
C,c>0
such that

c\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\leq\int_{|z|>\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(z)\quad\text{$\forall\,\varepsilon>0$}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\leq\frac{C}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}.

The estimates on the heat kernel and Green function still hold
[
108
, Theorem 2]
with
f(x,y)=f(|y-x|)=\,\mathrm{d}\mu/\,\mathrm{d}z
, see also the recent
[
88
]
.

Proof of Lemma
7.8
.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Follows by
[
46
, Theorem 1.2]
with
\rho(x,y)=|x-y|
,
V(r)=r^{N}
,
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=0
,
\psi(r)=1
, and
\phi_{1}(r)=r^{\alpha}
.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii). By direct computations,

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{|x-x_{0}|^{\alpha}}t|x-x_{0}|^{-(N+\alpha)}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{|x-x_{0}|^{\alpha}}^{\infty}t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\lesssim|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}.\qed\end{split}

7.2.
Combinations of assumption (
2
)

Sometimes the Green function has different power behaviours at zero and at infinity. As demonstrated in Theorem
3.3
, such a case leads to the estimate

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-N\theta_{\alpha}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta_{\alpha}}+t^{-N\theta_{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{2\theta_{2}}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$}

for weak dual solutions of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. Let us provide some concrete examples of operators
-\mathfrak{L}
in (
GPME
) whose Green functions satisfy combinations of (
2
).

We start with one of the most basic operators giving such an estimate:

Lemma 7.10
.

Assume
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
-\mathfrak{L}=(-\Delta)+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}=:(-\Delta)+(-\mathcal{L}^{\mu})
. Given the following statements:

(i)

For some constant
C>0
, we consider

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)=\frac{C}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C\begin{cases}f(x-x_{0},t)+\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\qquad\qquad&\text{if $0<t<|x-x_{0}|^{2}\leq 1$,}\\
\mathbb{H}_{-\Delta}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\qquad\qquad&\text{if $|x-x_{0}|^{2}<t<|x-x_{0}|^{\alpha}\leq 1,$}\\
\mathbb{H}_{-\Delta}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\qquad\qquad&\text{if $|x-x_{0}|^{\alpha}\leq t\leq 1$,}\\
\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\qquad\qquad&\text{if $t\geq 1$ or $|x-x_{0}|\geq 1$,}\end{cases}

where
f(x-x_{0},t):=(4\pi t)^{-N/2}\textup{exp}(-|x-x_{0}|^{2}/(16t))
.

(iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq C\begin{cases}|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-2)}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $|x|\leq 1$,}\\
|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}\qquad\qquad&\text{if $|x|>1$.}\\
\end{cases}

We have (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii).

Remark 7.11
.

Note that when
0<R\leq 1
, we get

\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim R^{2},\qquad\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\lesssim R^{-(N-2)}\quad\text{for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})$,}

and when
R>1
,

\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim R^{\alpha},\qquad\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\lesssim R^{-(N-\alpha)}\quad\text{for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})$.}

Hence, we are in the setting of Theorem
3.3
although in this example the small time behaviour is governed by the Laplacian and the large time by the fractional Laplacian.

Proof of Lemma
7.10
.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Follows by
[
105
, Theorem 2.13]
.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii). Assume
|x|\leq 1
. Then

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\bigg(\int_{0}^{|x|^{2}}+\int_{|x|^{2}}^{|x|^{\alpha}}+\int_{|x|^{\alpha}}^{1}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\bigg)\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Let us start with the integral involving
f(x,t)
. The change of variables
|x|^{2}/(16t)\mapsto t
gives

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{|x|^{2}}f(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\eqsim\int_{0}^{|x|^{2}}t^{-N/2}\textup{e}^{\frac{-|x|^{2}}{16t}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\Big(\frac{|x|^{2}}{16}\Big)^{-\frac{N}{2}+1}\int_{\frac{1}{16}}^{\infty}\tau^{\frac{N}{2}-2}\textup{e}^{-\tau}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\eqsim|x|^{-(N-2)},\end{split}

where we estimated the final integral by (
7.1
). The integrals involving
\mathbb{H}_{-\Delta}^{0}
can be estimated in a similar way. It remains to estimate the contribution from
\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}
:

\begin{split}&\bigg(\int_{0}^{|x|^{2}}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\bigg)\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\bigg(\int_{0}^{|x|^{2}}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\bigg)\min\{t^{-N/\alpha},t|x|^{-N-\alpha}\}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{|x|^{2}}t|x|^{-N-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{-N/\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}t\eqsim|x|^{-(N-2)+2-\alpha}+1.\end{split}

Since
|x|\leq 1
, we have
|x|^{-(N-2)+2-\alpha}\leq|x|^{-(N-2)}
and
1\leq|x|^{-(N-2)}
.

Assume
|x|>1
. Then

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim|x|^{-(N-\alpha)}.\end{split}

We combine the results to complete the proof.
∎

Let us now consider the relativistic Schrödinger type operators like

(7.2)

(\kappa^{2}I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-\kappa^{\alpha}I\qquad\text{with $\kappa>0$ and $\alpha\in(0,2)$}

are Lévy operators
[
100
, Lemma 2]
(see also
[
79
]
and
[
72
, Appendix B]
), i.e., they can be written on the form
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
, see (
1.1
), with a measure satisfying (
1
).

Lemma 7.12
.

Assume
-\mathfrak{L}
is given by (
7.2
). Given the following statements, for
\alpha\in(0,2)
:

(i)

For some constant
C>0
, we consider

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)=\frac{C}{|z|^{\frac{N+\alpha}{2}}}K_{\frac{N+\alpha}{2}}(\kappa|z|),

where
K_{a}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind with index
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all
\gamma>2-\alpha
,

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C\min\bigg\{t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}},\frac{t}{|x-x_{0}|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x-x_{0}|)^{\gamma}}\bigg\}\qquad\text{if $0<t<1$,}

and

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C\min\bigg\{t^{-\frac{N}{2}},\frac{t}{|x-x_{0}|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x-x_{0}|)^{\gamma}}\bigg\}\qquad\text{if $t>1$.}

(iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq C\big(|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}+|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-2)}\big).

We have (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii).

Remark 7.13
.

It is interesting to note that

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\eqsim\frac{1}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\quad\text{as $|z|\to 0$}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\eqsim\frac{1}{|z|^{\frac{N+\alpha+1}{2}}}\textup{e}^{-\kappa|z|}\quad\text{as $|z|\to\infty$.}

Proof of Lemma
7.12
.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Follows by
[
109
, Section 5]
.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii). Since

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

we will have to consider three cases

{\rm(I)}\qquad\frac{t}{|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}>t\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}<1,

{\rm(II)}\qquad\frac{t}{|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}=t\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}=1,

and

{\rm(III)}\qquad\frac{t}{|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}<t\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}>1.

In the case of (I), we have three different behaviours:

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x)&\eqsim\int_{0}^{|x|^{\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma\frac{\alpha}{N+\alpha}}}\frac{t}{|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{|x|^{\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma\frac{\alpha}{N+\alpha}}}^{1}t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{-\frac{N}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\frac{1}{2}|x|^{-(N-\alpha)}(1+|x|)^{-\gamma\frac{N-\alpha}{N+\alpha}}+\frac{\alpha}{N-\alpha}|x|^{-(N-\alpha)}(1+|x|)^{-\gamma\frac{N-\alpha}{N+\alpha}}-\frac{\alpha}{N-\alpha}+\frac{2}{N-2}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\frac{N-\alpha}{N+\alpha}|x|^{-(N-\alpha)}(1+|x|)^{-\gamma\frac{N-\alpha}{N+\alpha}}+\frac{N(2-\alpha)}{(N-2)(N-\alpha)}\\
&\leq\Big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{N-\alpha}{N+\alpha}+\frac{N(2-\alpha)}{(N-2)(N-\alpha)}\Big)|x|^{-(N-\alpha)}\qquad\text{in $\{x\,:\,|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}<1\}$,}\end{split}

were we used
1+|x|\geq 1
to get

\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{\gamma\frac{N-\alpha}{N+\alpha}}}\leq 1.

In the case of (II), we have two different behaviours:

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x)&\eqsim\int_{0}^{1}t\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{-\frac{N}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{N-2}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{N-2}{N+2}.\end{split}

In the case of (III), we have two different behaviours:

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x)&\eqsim\int_{0}^{|x|^{(N+\alpha)\frac{2}{N+2}}(1+|x|)^{\gamma\frac{2}{N+2}}}\frac{t}{|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{|x|^{(N+\alpha)\frac{2}{N+2}}(1+|x|)^{\gamma\frac{2}{N+2}}}^{\infty}t^{-\frac{N}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\frac{1}{2}|x|^{-(N-2)}\Big(\frac{|x|^{2-\alpha}}{(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}\Big)^{\frac{N-2}{N+2}}+\frac{2}{N-2}|x|^{-(N-2)}\Big(\frac{|x|^{2-\alpha}}{(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}\Big)^{\frac{N-2}{N+2}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\frac{N-2}{N+2}|x|^{-(N-2)}\Big(\frac{|x|^{2-\alpha}}{(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}\Big)^{\frac{N-2}{N+2}}.\end{split}

By the assumption
\gamma>2-\alpha
, we get

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x)\lesssim|x|^{-(N-2)}\qquad\text{in $\{x\,:\,|x|^{N+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{\gamma}>1\}$.}\end{split}

We then collect the three cases in one estimate to complete the proof.
∎

Finally, we also consider the generator of a finite range isotropically symmetric
\alpha
-stable process in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with jumps of size larger than
1
removed.

Lemma 7.14
.

Assume
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with a measure
\mu
satisfying (
1
). Given the following statements, for
\alpha\in(0,2)
:

(i)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)=\frac{C}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq 1}.

(ii)

There exist constants
C,c>0
and
0<C_{*},R_{*}<1
such that,

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C\min\big\{t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}},t|x-x_{0}|^{-(N+\alpha)}\big\},\qquad\text{$0<t<R_{*}^{\alpha}$, $|x-x_{0}|\leq R_{*}$,}

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C\exp\Big(-c|x-x_{0}|\log\Big(\frac{|x-x_{0}|}{t}\Big)\Big),\qquad\text{$|x-x_{0}|\geq\max\{t/C_{*},R_{*}\}$,}

and

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{2}}\exp\Big(-c\frac{|x-x_{0}|^{2}}{t}\Big),\qquad\text{$t>R_{*}^{\alpha}$, $|x-x_{0}|\leq t/C_{*}$.}

(iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq C\big(|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-\alpha)}+|x-x_{0}|^{-(N-2)}\big).

We have (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii).

Remark 7.15
.

We are again in the setting of Theorem
3.3
by Remark
7.11
.

Proof of Lemma
7.14
.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Follows by
[
44
, Proposition 2.1 and Theorem 2.3]
.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii). Follows by the proof of
[
44
, Theorem 4.7]
.
∎

7.3.
On the assumption (
2
)

If the Green function decays fast enough at infinity, the function itself will not only be
L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}
but indeed
L^{1}
, see (
2
). As demonstrated in Theorem
3.7
, such a case leads to the estimate

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-1/(m-1)}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$},
