for weak dual solutions of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. Let us provide some concrete examples of operators
-\mathfrak{L}
in (
GPME
) whose Green functions satisfy (
2
). Actually, any operator of the form
I-\mathfrak{L}
has a Green function satisfying (
2
). In what follows, we will explain this result, and illustrate it with other examples as well.

Lemma 7.16
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
7.1
,

\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 1.

Hence, the operator
I-\mathfrak{L}
has a Green function which satisfies (
2
).

Proof.

Assumption (
2
), an application of the Tonelli lemma, and the fact that, by Remark
4.2
(i.e., decay of
L^{1}
-norm),
\int\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(\cdot,t)=\int\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\leq 1
for every fixed
t>0
concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

We immediately see that the presence of the identity operator is crucial. In fact, if
-\mathfrak{L}
is such that the corresponding heat equation preserves mass, then
\|\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\infty
(cf. Proposition
7.1
). Examples of mass preserving operators are Lévy operators (
1.1
) with
c=0
.

Let us begin by considering the extreme case
-\mathfrak{L}=I
for which the PDE in (
GPME
) reads

\partial_{t}u=-u^{m}.

For any function
t\mapsto Y(t)
, that equation is an ODE of the form

Y^{\prime}(t)=-Y(t)^{1+(m-1)}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad Y(t)\leq\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}.

Hence, by the comparison principle for (
GPME
) with
-\mathfrak{L}=I
(where we take
Y(0)=\infty
), we get the absolute bound

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Y(t)\leq\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}.

See also Section III.C in
[
117
]
. The above is also contained in the following lemma:

Lemma 7.18
.

The identity operator
-\mathfrak{L}=I
has a Green function which satisfies (
2
), i.e.,

\|\mathbb{G}_{I}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{1}<\infty.

Proof.

We obtain

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{H}_{I}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{H}_{I}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x,

i.e.,
\int\mathbb{H}_{I}^{x_{0}}(\cdot,t)=\textup{e}^{-t}
for all
t>0
. By the definition of the Green function (cf. Proposition
7.1
), we conclude.
∎

Remark 7.19
.

(a)

The proof also demonstrates that the operator
-\mathfrak{L}=I
is not conserving mass. Indeed, in the corresponding heat equation, it decays with time.

(b)

Moreover, it provides a trivial example of an operator which yields boundedness in the nonlinear case
(m>1)
, but not in the linear case (
m=1
).

Of course, we can reapply the same strategy of comparison with
Y(t)
for (
GPME
) with
-\mathfrak{L}\mapsto I-\mathfrak{L}
to get

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq Y(t)\leq\Big(\frac{1}{(m-1)t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}

independently of
\mathfrak{L}
!

Remark 7.20
.

When
m=1
, we need to adapt another strategy (since
Y(t)=Y(0)\textup{e}^{-t})
, but recall that by defining

u(x,t):=\textup{e}^{-t}v(x,t)

where
v
solves (
GPME
) with
m=1
, then
u
solves

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathfrak{L}[u]+u=0\qquad\qquad&\text{in}\qquad\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(0,T],\\
u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}\qquad\qquad&\text{on}\qquad\mathbb{R}^{N}.\end{cases}

Hence, in this case,
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing follows as long as it holds for
v
, i.e., as long as
\mathfrak{L}
is strong enough to provide it.

When
\kappa=1
in (
7.2
), we get the Lévy operator

-\mathcal{L}^{\mu_{\text{RS}}}:=(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-I,

i.e.,
(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
is of the form
(I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu_{\text{RS}}})
. Hence:

Lemma 7.21
.

The operator
-\mathfrak{L}=(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
with
\alpha\in(0,2)
has a Green function which satisfies (
2
), i.e.,

\|\mathbb{G}_{(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{1}<\infty.

Remark 7.22
.

The operator
-\mathfrak{L}=(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
with
\alpha=1
appears e.g. in
[
6
]
for the linear equation (
GPME
) with
m=1
. Since the mentioned operator is of the form
(I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu_{\text{RS}}})
,
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing holds whenever it holds for
\mathcal{L}^{\mu_{\text{RS}}}
(see Remark
7.20
). The heat kernel bounds of Lemma
7.12
then provides the result through Theorem
6.1
. In the nonlinear case (
m>1
), however, the above lemma ensures that (
2
) holds and we deduce absolute bounds.

Proof of Lemma
7.21
.

Note that

\mathbb{G}_{(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x)=\mathscr{F}^{-1}\big[(1+|\cdot|^{2})^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\big](x-x_{0}),

i.e., the Bessel potential. The result then follows by Proposition 2 in Chapter V.3 in
[
106
]
. Or, we can simply note that
\mathbb{G}_{(I-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}}^{x_{0}}=\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu_{\text{RS}}}}^{x_{0}}
and
-\mathcal{L}^{\mu_{\text{RS}}}
is such that the heat equation has
L^{1}
-decay, cf. Lemma
7.16
.
∎

Again, the operator related to the Bessel potential does not conserve mass since
(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-I
does so. Moreover, in the latter case, assumption (
2
) cannot hold (cf. Remark
7.17
) and we can write the PDE in (
GPME
) as

\partial_{t}u+(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}[u^{m}]=u^{m}.

This is in contrast with the operators
I-\mathfrak{L}
in Lemma
7.16
which satisfies the PDE

\partial_{t}u-\mathfrak{L}[u^{m}]=-u^{m}.

Taking
-\mathfrak{L}=(I-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
in the latter, we see that assumption (
2
) relies on either the (strong) absorption term being present or the operator itself being
positive
, or also both being present of course.

7.4.
On the assumption (
2
)

By Corollary
D.6
,
\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
has at least as good integrability properties as
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}
. It is, moreover, always defined for descent operators
-\mathfrak{L}
, see the discussion in Section
D
. There should therefore be no surprise that assumption (
2
) is quite general, however, as shown in Theorem
3.5
, it provides a rather poor smoothing estimate:

(7.3)

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}+\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$,}

for weak dual solutions of (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}
. Let us provide some concrete examples of operators
-\mathfrak{L}
in (
GPME
) whose Green functions satisfy (
2
), and let us also see how to improve the above estimate. To continue, we advice the reader to recall (
4.5
).

Lemma 7.23
.

The fractional Laplacian/Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
with
\alpha\in(0,2]
has a Green function which satisfies (
2
), i.e.,

\|\mathbb{G}_{I+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{p}<\infty

for some
p\in(1,N/(N-\alpha))
.

Proof.

We use (
7.1
) with
\vartheta:=\frac{N}{\alpha}-1
to obtain

(7.4)

\begin{split}&\|\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\textup{e}^{-t|\xi|^{\alpha}}]\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textup{e}^{-t|\xi|^{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\\
&\eqsim\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-tr^{\alpha}}r^{N-1}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-tr}r^{\vartheta}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{1}{\alpha}t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\Gamma\Big(\frac{N}{\alpha}\Big),\end{split}

and hence, by Remark
4.2
(i.e.,
L^{1}
-decay for
m=1
),

\begin{split}\|\mathbb{G}_{I+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\|\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\tau^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-\tau r}r^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}r,\end{split}

which is finite if
p<N/(N-\alpha)
due to (
7.1
) again.
∎

The fractional Laplacian, however, satisfies our strongest assumption (
2
) as well. We will therefore consider an operator which satisfies (
2
), but for which it is not possible to verify (
2
) or (
2
). To that end, consider the sum of onedimensional fractional Laplacians:

(7.5)

-\mathfrak{L}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(-\partial_{x_{i}x_{i}}^{2})^{\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2}}\qquad\text{with $\alpha_{i}\in(0,2)$.}

It can be written on the form
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with
\mu
, for some constant
C>0
, given by

\,\mathrm{d}\mu(z)=C\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{1+\alpha_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}z_{i}\prod_{j\neq i}\,\mathrm{d}\delta_{0}(z_{j}).

This measure satisfies (
1
) since each onedimensional fractional Laplacian measure does, and we have:

Lemma 7.24
.

Assume
\mathfrak{L}
is given by (
7.5
) and

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}>1\qquad\text{where $\alpha_{i}\in(0,2)$}.

Then

\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{p}<\infty

for some

p\in\Big(1,\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}-1}\Big).

Remark 7.25
.

(a)

Note that if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}>1\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\frac{N}{\alpha}>1.

We thus recover the condition of Lemma
7.23
.

(b)

Various extensions within the framework of anisotropic fractional Laplacians can be found in
[
19
,
110
,
118
,
20
]
.

(c)

By
[
9
, Section 3]
,

(7.6)

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{H}_{(-\partial_{x_{i}x_{i}}^{2})^{\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq C\prod_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}^{x_{0,i}}(x_{i},t),

where

\rho_{i}^{x_{0,i}}(x_{i},t)=\min\big\{t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}},t|x_{i}-x_{0,i}|^{-(1+\alpha_{i})}\big\}.

However, the example stated at the end of
[
18
]
shows that for
\alpha_{i}=\alpha
with
\alpha\leq(N-1)/2<N
, small times (hence all times), and the choice
x=\xi e_{1}
with
\xi>0
, yields
\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x,t)=\infty
. We thus conclude that at least in this case, it is not possible to verify the second parts of (
2
) or (
2
).

(d)

Solutions of (
GPME
) with
-\mathfrak{L}
defined by (
7.5
) satisfy (
7.3
). Once this estimate is established, we can, moreover, use the scaling of the operator to get it on an invariant form. We restrict to the case
\alpha_{i}=\alpha
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. As in Remark
3.2
,
if
u
solves (
GPME
), then

u_{\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda}(x,t):=\kappa u(\Xi x,\Lambda t)\qquad\text{for all $\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda>0$}

also solves (
GPME
) as long as
\kappa^{m-1}\Xi^{\alpha}=\Lambda
. This means that

\|u_{\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim t^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}+\|u_{\kappa,\Xi,\Lambda}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}

or

\|u(\Xi\cdot,\Lambda t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\lesssim\Xi^{\frac{\alpha}{m-1}}(\Lambda t)^{-\frac{1}{m-1}}+\Xi^{-N}\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

The choice
\Xi=\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{(m-1)\theta}(\Lambda t)^{\theta}
and
\Lambda t\mapsto t
, then gives

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\frac{C}{t^{N\theta}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\alpha\theta}\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$},

where
\theta=(\alpha+N(m-1))^{-1}
and
C
now depending on
C_{p}
instead of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
.
11
11

11

In the case
\alpha_{i}=\alpha
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, the bilinear form of the operator (
7.5
) is comparable to the bilinear form of the fractional Laplacian, and one could instead use the Sobolev inequality for the latter operator (see e.g.
[
56
]
) together with a Moser iteration to obtain the
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing.
This in turn implies the corresponding Nash inequality (see Section
6.2
). Note that, even in the case
\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{j}
, one can deduce the Sobolev inequality, from which the Nash inequality follows, by scratch
[
43
, Theorem 2.4]
. This will then ensure the
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing estimate both in the linear and nonlinear case.

Proof of Lemma
7.24
.

Recall that the heat kernel is given by (
7.6
). Since we are considering a Lévy operator, it provides
L^{1}
-decay, and by (
7.4
) with
N=1
, we get

\begin{split}&\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\bigg\|\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{H}_{(-\partial_{x_{i}x_{i}}^{2})^{\alpha_{i}/2}}^{0}(\cdot_{i},t)\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\|\mathbb{H}_{(-\partial_{x_{i}x_{i}}^{2})^{\alpha_{i}/2}}^{0}(\cdot_{i},t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\bigg(\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}}\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\Big)\bigg)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\eqsim\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}t^{-\frac{p-1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Again, by (
7.1
), the result follows.
∎

Note that we have exploited the fact that
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)
indeed has the on-diagonal upper bound
t^{-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}}
, see e.g. Corollary 3.2 in
[
118
]
. Since the off-diagonal bound cannot give a useful Green function estimate (in all cases), we resort to our assumption (
2
). We refer the reader to Remark
6.2
which provides various examples of on-diagonal bounds.

Let us turn our attention to another interesting example where only a useful on-diagonal bound can be deduced:

Lemma 7.26
.

Assume
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with a measure
\mu
satisfying (
1
) and, for
\alpha\in(0,2)
and constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
,

\frac{C_{1}}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq 1}\leq\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq 1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\leq C_{3}\mathbf{1}_{|z|>1}.

Then, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\leq Ct^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\textup{e}^{t},

and, moreover,

\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{p}<\infty

for some
p\in(1,N/(N-\alpha))
.

Proof.

The estimate on the heat kernel follows by the beginning of Section 2 in
[
45
]
with
V(r)=r^{N}
and
\phi(r)=r^{\alpha}
, and since the heat kernel is proven to be Hölder continuous in Section 3 of the same reference (so that the exceptional set is empty). Note that the proof uses that
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)=\textup{e}^{t}\mathbb{H}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)
. We then get

\begin{split}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\eqsim\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\big(t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}}\textup{e}^{t}\big)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-\frac{t}{p}}t^{-\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Again, by (
7.1
), the result follows.
∎

7.5.
A nonexample of our theory

We will now consider a Lévy operator which does not satisfy any of (
2
)–(
2
).

Consider the generator of a subordinate Brownian motion with Fourier symbol
\phi(|\xi|^{2})
where
\phi(\lambda):=\log(1+\lambda^{\frac{\alpha}{2}})
. This process is known as a rotationally invariant
geometric

\alpha
-stable process, see Section 5 in
[
17
]
.

Lemma 7.27
.

Assume
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with a measure
\mu
satisfying (
1
). If
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
has Fourier symbol given by

\log(1+|\xi|^{\alpha}),

then the heat kernel is given by

\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{x_{0}}(x,s)s^{t-1}\textup{e}^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Moreover,

\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq C_{1}<\infty.

Remark 7.28
.

The operator is indeed a Lévy operator, therefore it is not surprising that it provides
L^{1}
-decay. It is, moreover, worth noting that Theorems 5.45 and 5.46 in
[
17
]
establish that the density of the Lévy measure corresponding to the rotationally invariant geometric
\alpha
-stable process satisfies

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\eqsim\frac{1}{|z|^{N}}\quad\text{as $|z|\to 0$}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)\eqsim\frac{1}{|z|^{N+\alpha}}\quad\text{as $|z|\to\infty$}.

In fact,

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\,\mathrm{d}z}(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(z,s)s^{-1}\textup{e}^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}s,

see equation (5.69) in
[
17
]
.

Proof of Lemma
7.27
.

The formula for the heat kernel is given by equation (5.68) in
[
17
]
. Moreover, since
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
actually provides conservation of mass, we get

\begin{split}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\frac{1}{\Gamma(t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\mathbb{H}_{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{0}(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}s^{t-1}\textup{e}^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\frac{1}{\Gamma(t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{t-1}\textup{e}^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\frac{\Gamma(t)}{\Gamma(t)}\,\mathrm{d}t=1.\qed\end{split}

The proof also demonstrates that (
2
) cannot hold, and moreover, neither can (
2
), (
2
), and (
2
):

Lemma 7.29
.

Assume
\mathfrak{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
with a measure
\mu
satisfying (
1
). If
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
has Fourier symbol given by

\log(1+|\xi|^{\alpha}),

then there is some
R>0
such that

\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x>CR^{\alpha}\qquad\text{for all $\alpha\in(0,2)$ and all $C>0$,}

and

\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{x_{0}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\infty\qquad\text{for all $p>1$.}

Proof.

By Theorem 5.35 in
[
17
]
,

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x)\eqsim|x-x_{0}|^{-N}\big(-\log(|x|^{2}))^{-2}\eqsim|x-x_{0}|^{-N}\big(\log(|x|))^{-2}\qquad\text{as $|x|\to 0$.}

Then for small enough
0<R<<1
,

\int_{B_{R}(0)}\mathbb{G}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\eqsim\int_{0}^{R}r^{-1}(\log(r))^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}r\eqsim\Big(\log\Big(\frac{1}{R}\Big)\Big)^{-1}.

Now, the statement

\Big(\log\Big(\frac{1}{R}\Big)\Big)^{-1}>CR^{\alpha}\qquad\text{for some $0<R<<1$}

is equivalent to

\frac{1}{R}<\textup{exp}\Big(C\Big(\frac{1}{R}\Big)^{\alpha}\Big)\qquad\text{for some $0<R<<1$,}

which is clearly true for all
\alpha\in(0,2)
and all
C>0
.

We already know that

\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t.

By Theorem 5.5.2 in
[
17
]
, for all
0<t\leq\min\{1,N/(2\alpha)\}
,

\begin{split}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{p}&\geq Ct^{p}\bigg(\int_{|x|<1}|x|^{-p(N-t\alpha)}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{|x|>1}|x|^{-p(N+\alpha)}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)\\
&\gtrsim t^{p}\int_{0}^{1}r^{N-1-p(N-t\alpha)}\,\mathrm{d}r.\end{split}

If
1<p\leq 2
, then

\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}\leq\min\Big\{1,\frac{N}{2\alpha}\Big\},

and

\begin{split}\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\geq\int_{0}^{\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\gtrsim\int_{0}^{\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}}\textup{e}^{-t}t\bigg(t^{p}\lim_{\xi\to 0^{+}}\int_{\xi}^{1}r^{N-1-p(N-t\alpha)}\,\mathrm{d}r\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}}\textup{e}^{-t}t^{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{p\alpha}\frac{1}{\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{(p-1)}{p}-t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\bigg(\lim_{\xi\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\xi^{p\alpha(\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}-t)}}-1\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\geq\int_{0}^{\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}}\textup{e}^{-t}t^{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{N(p-1)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\bigg(\lim_{\xi\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\xi^{p\alpha(\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}-t)}}-1\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\infty.\end{split}

If
p>2
, then

\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{p-1}{p}>\frac{N}{\alpha}\frac{1}{2},

and we simply consider

\|\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\geq\int_{0}^{\min\{1,N/(2\alpha)\}}\textup{e}^{-t}\|\mathbb{H}_{-\mathcal{L}^{\mu}}^{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,\mathrm{d}t

to reach the same conclusion.
∎

Appendix A
Technical lemmas

Implicitly, we use the following in the Moser iteration:

Lemma A.1
.

Assume
K(p)>0
is such that
\lim_{p\to\infty}K(p)<\infty
,
p_{0}\geq 1
, and

\psi\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|\psi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq K(p)\quad\text{for all $p\in[p_{0},\infty)$.}

Then
\psi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and moreover,

\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\lim_{p\to\infty}K(p).

Proof.

Define
K(\infty):=\lim_{p\to\infty}K(p)
, and consider

\Psi:=|\psi|\mathbf{1}_{\{|\psi|\leq K(\infty)+1\}}+(K(\infty)+1)\mathbf{1}_{\{|\psi|>K(\infty)+1\}}=\min\{|\psi|,K(\infty)+1\}

from which it follows that
\Psi\leq K(\infty)+1
and
\Psi\leq|\psi|
. Then
\Psi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
\|\Psi\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|\psi\|_{L^{p}}\leq K(p)
for all
p\in[p_{0},\infty)
, and hence,
\|\Psi\|_{L^{\infty}}=\lim_{p\to\infty}\|\Psi\|_{L^{p}}\leq K(\infty)
. But then
\min\{|\psi|,K(\infty)+1\}\leq K(\infty)
which implies
\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq K(\infty)
.
∎

The next lemma is classical, but we state and prove it for completeness.

Lemma A.2

(A DeGiorgi-type lemma)
.

Assume that
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and that
f\in L^{\infty}(B_{3R}(z))
with
R>0
fixed. If, for any
R\leq\rho<\bar{\rho}\leq 3R
, some
\delta\in(0,1)
, and some
M>0
independent of
\rho,\bar{\rho}
, we have that

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho}(z))}\leq\delta\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\bar{\rho}}(z))}+M,

then

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho}(z))}\leq\frac{1}{1-\delta}M.

Proof.

We follow the proof of Lemma 1.2 in Chapter 4 of
[
80
]
. Fix
\rho\geq R
. For some
0<\eta<1
we consider the sequence
\{\rho_{i}\}
defined recursively by

\rho_{0}=\rho\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{i+1}:=\rho_{i}+(1-\eta)\eta^{i}\rho.

Note that
\rho_{\infty}=2\rho
.
Since
2\rho=\rho_{\infty}>\ldots>\rho_{1}>\rho_{0}=\rho
,

\begin{split}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho}(z))}&=\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho_{0}}(z))}\leq\delta\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho_{1}}(z))}+M\leq\delta^{2}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho_{2}}(z))}+(1+\delta)M\\
&\leq\ldots\leq\delta^{k}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho_{k}}(z))}+M\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\delta^{i}.\end{split}

The conclusion follows by letting
k\to\infty
.
∎

Appendix B
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing controls
L^{q}
–
L^{p}
-smoothing

Throughout this section,
F>0
is some nonincreasing function and
C>0
is some constant (which might change from line to line) not depending on any norm of
u
.

Theorem B.1
.

Assume that
0\leq u_{0}=u(\cdot,0)\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and that, for a.e.
t_{1}>t_{0}\geq 0
,

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq F(t_{1}-t_{0})\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{q}}^{\gamma}\qquad\text{for some $0\leq\gamma<1$ and $q\in[1,\infty)$.}

Then, for all
1\leq q\leq p\leq\infty
,

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{p}}\leq G(t_{1}-t_{0})H(\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{q}}),

where
G,H\geq 0
are functions depending on
F,\gamma,p,q
and
\gamma,p,q
, respectively, which has to be determined in each case.

The proof is a consequence of several results in this section. We start by investigating immediate consequences of
L^{q}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing effects through Young and Hölder inequalities, in addition to a DeGiorgi type lemma.

Lemma B.2
.

Assume that
0\leq u_{0}=u(\cdot,0)\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and that, for a.e.
t_{1}>t_{0}\geq 0
,

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq F(t_{1}-t_{0})\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{q}}^{\gamma}\qquad\text{for some $0\leq\gamma<1$ and $q\in[1,\infty)$.}

Then:

(a)

(
L^{\leq q}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing)

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq CF(t_{1}-t_{0})^{\frac{q}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{\gamma r}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}\qquad\text{for $r\in[1,q]$.}

(b)

(
L^{\leq q}
–
L^{>q}
-smoothing)

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{p}}\leq CF(t_{1}-t_{0})^{\frac{q}{p}\frac{p-r}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{r}{p}\frac{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma p}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}\qquad\text{for $p\in(r,\infty)$ and $r\in[1,q]$.}

Remark B.3
.

The homogeneous smoothing estimates (cf. Theorem
3.1
(a) and Theorem
3.7
) can respectively be recovered by choosing
q=1=r
and:

(i)

If
\gamma=\alpha\theta
, then

\frac{\gamma(p-1)+1}{p}=\frac{1}{p}\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{p}}.

(ii)

If
\gamma=0
, then

\frac{\gamma(p-1)+1}{p}=\frac{1}{p}.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

(a) Since
q\geq r\geq 1
, we have

\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{q}}^{\gamma}\leq\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{\gamma(q-r)}{q}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{\gamma r}{q}}.

Applying the Young inequality (
1.7
) with
\vartheta=\frac{q}{\gamma(q-r)}>1
yields

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{\gamma(q-r)}{q}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}}+CF(t_{1}-t_{0})^{\frac{q}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{\gamma r}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}.

Since
\gamma(q-r)/q<1
, we can reabsorb the
L^{\infty}
-norm by a variant of a classical lemma due to DeGiorgi (cf. Lemma
A.2
).

(b) When
p\geq r\geq 1
, we use Proposition
4.1
(b)(ii) to get

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{p-r}{p}}\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{r}{p}}\leq\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{p-r}{p}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{r}{p}}.

Now, part (a) yields

\begin{split}\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{p}}&\leq CF(t_{1}-t_{0})^{\frac{q}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}\frac{p-r}{p}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{\gamma r}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}\frac{p-r}{p}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{r}{p}}\\
&=CF(t_{1}-t_{0})^{\frac{q}{p}\frac{p-r}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{r}{p}\frac{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma p}{(1-\gamma)q+\gamma r}}.\end{split}

The proof is complete.
∎

As we saw, the easy consequences of smoothing effects, is to loose integrability on the right-hand side. Now, we instead want to gain integrability, i.e., we want
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
to
L^{\geq 1}
–
L^{\infty}
.

To gain integrability, however, requires a refined technique. In bounded domains
|\Omega|<\infty
, this is usually accomplished by the fact that
L^{\tilde{q}}\subseteq L^{q}
with
1\leq q\leq\tilde{q}\leq\infty
, i.e., by the Hölder inequality

\|u\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}^{q}=\int_{\Omega}|u|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\bigg(\int_{\Omega}\big(|u|^{q}\big)^{\frac{\tilde{q}}{q}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{q}{\tilde{q}}}\bigg(\int_{\Omega}\big(1\big)^{\frac{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{q}-q}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{\frac{\tilde{q}-q}{\tilde{q}}}=\|u\|_{L^{\tilde{q}}(\Omega)}^{q}|\Omega|^{\frac{\tilde{q}-q}{\tilde{q}}}.

So, while such a statement is trivial in bounded domains, the story is quite different in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. The reason for this can be seen by the following estimate:

\|u\|_{L^{q}}^{q}=\int_{B_{R}(0)}|u|^{q}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(0)}|u|^{q}.

On the small ball, we use that
L^{\tilde{q}}\subseteq L^{q}
, but what to do on the complementary set? In fact, there the “natural” ordering is

\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{\tilde{q}}}\leq\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{q}},

i.e., opposite of the small ball. Hence,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(0)}|u|^{q}\leq C_{R}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}(0)}|u|^{\tilde{q}}\bigg)^{\frac{q}{\tilde{q}}}

cannot be true for all functions, and must be a property of the equation itself.

We therefore rely on a nice idea taken from Section 3.1 in
[
113
]
: Consider (
GPME
) with the nonlinearity

\varphi:r\mapsto(r+\varepsilon)^{m}-\varepsilon^{m}\qquad\text{for some $\varepsilon>0$ and some $m>1$.}

In the case of Lévy operators (
1.1
) with
c=0
, this equation has been studied in
[
63
]
, and in the case of the more general setting of possibly
x
-dependent
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive operators, this equation has been studied in detail in e.g.
[
22
, Appendix B]
and
[
57
]
. E.g., existence, uniqueness, and the comparison principle holds for sign-changing solutions. In our setting, we also have:

Lemma B.4
.

Assume (

H
m

) and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
u
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with initial data
0\leq u_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and
v
be a weak dual solution of (
GPME
) with nonlinearity
\varphi
and initial data
0\leq v_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Under suitable assumptions on the associated Green function:

(i)
For a.e.
t_{1}>t_{0}\geq 0
,

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq F(t_{1}-t_{0})\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{1}}^{\gamma}\qquad\text{for some $0\leq\gamma<1$.}

If, moreover,
u(x,t)\leq v(x,t)+\varepsilon
for a.e.
(x,t)\in Q_{T}
, then:

(ii)
For a.e.
t_{1}>t_{0}\geq 0
,

\|v(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq F(t_{1}-t_{0})\|v(t_{0})\|_{L^{1}}^{\gamma}+C\varepsilon,

with the same
F,\gamma
as given above.

Proof.

(i) This already holds, see Theorems
3.1
and
3.7
.

(ii) Even though the nonlinearity
\varphi(r)
is different from
r^{m}
, we will see that we can repeat the steps leading up to Theorems
3.1
and
3.7
when we in addition know that
u\leq v+\varepsilon
.

Recall that by Lemma
4.4
, we have

t\mapsto t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}u^{m}(\cdot,t)\qquad\text{is nondecreasing for a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$,}

and since
u\leq v+\varepsilon
, we get the following time-monotonicity for
v+\varepsilon
:

t\mapsto t^{\frac{m}{m-1}}(v(\cdot,t)+\varepsilon)^{m}\qquad\text{is nondecreasing for a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

Then

\begin{split}&\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}(v(x_{0},t)+\varepsilon)^{m}\,\mathrm{d}t-\varepsilon^{m}(\tau-\tau_{*})=\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}\varphi(v(x_{0},t))\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}v(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}v(x,\tau)\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}v(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

or

\begin{split}\int_{\tau_{*}}^{\tau}(v(x_{0},t)+\varepsilon)^{m}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}v(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\varepsilon^{m}(\tau-\tau_{*}).\end{split}

By the time-monotonicity,

(v(x_{0},\tau_{*})+\varepsilon)^{m}\leq\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}v(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+C(m)\varepsilon^{m}.

Since
r\mapsto r^{m}
is superadditive on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we also get

v^{m}(x_{0},\tau_{*})\leq\frac{C(m)}{\tau_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}v(x,\tau_{*})\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+(C(m)-1)\varepsilon^{m}.

Hence, we get the stated
L^{1}
–
L^{\infty}
-smoothing for
v
by simply following the proof for
u
, and using that
r\mapsto r^{\frac{1}{m}}
is subadditive on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
∎

Proposition B.5
.

Assume that
0\leq u_{0}=u(\cdot,0)\in(L^{1}\cap L^{p})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for some
p\in(1,\infty)
, and that, for a.e.
t_{1}>t_{0}\geq 0
,

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq F(t_{1}-t_{0})\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{1}}^{\gamma}\qquad\text{for some $\gamma\in[0,1)$.}

Moreover, if the comparison principle holds for sign-changing weak dual solutions of (
GPME
) with the nonlinearity
\varphi
, then

\|u(t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq CF(t_{1}-t_{0})^{\frac{1}{\gamma(p-1)+1}}\|u(t_{0})\|_{L^{p}}^{\frac{\gamma p}{\gamma(p-1)+1}}.

Remark B.6
.

The comparison principle indeed holds for sign-changing weak dual solutions of (
GPME
) with the nonlinearity
\varphi
: We simply repeat the existence proof in this setting.

Remark B.7
.

Let us check that we indeed recover the different homogeneous cases:

(i)

If
\gamma_{1}=\gamma=\alpha\theta
,
F(t)=Ct^{-\gamma_{2}}
, and
\gamma_{2}=N\theta
, then

\frac{\gamma_{1}p}{\gamma_{1}(p-1)+1}=\alpha p\theta_{p}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{1}(p-1)+1}=N\theta_{p}.

(ii)

If
\gamma_{1}=\gamma=0
,
F(t)=Ct^{-\gamma_{2}}
, and
\gamma_{2}=1/(m-1)
, then

\frac{\gamma_{1}p}{\gamma_{1}(p-1)+1}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{1}(p-1)+1}=\frac{1}{m-1}.

Proof of Proposition
B.5
.

Consider the function
v_{\varepsilon}:=u-\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon>0
, where
u
solves (
GPME
) with initial data
u_{0}\geq 0
. Note that
v_{\varepsilon}
also solves (
GPME
) with
\varphi
as nonlinearity (we have subtracted the term
\varepsilon^{m}
for normalization purposes), and unsigned initial data
u_{0}-\varepsilon
.

Now, consider the solution
\tilde{v}_{\varepsilon}
of (
GPME
) with nonlinearity
\varphi
and initial data
(u_{0}-\varepsilon)_{+}
. Due to the Hölder inequality,

\begin{split}\int(u_{0}-\varepsilon)_{+}=\int_{\{u_{0}>\varepsilon\}}(u_{0}-\varepsilon)\leq\int_{\{u_{0}>\varepsilon\}}u_{0}=\int u_{0}\mathbf{1}_{\{u_{0}>\varepsilon\}}\leq\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}|\{u_{0}>\varepsilon\}|^{\frac{p-1}{p}}.\end{split}

Moreover, for any
f\geq 0
,

\|f\|_{L^{p}}^{p}=\int_{\{0\leq f\leq\varepsilon\}}|f|^{p}+\int_{\{f>\varepsilon\}}|f|^{p}\geq\int_{\{f>\varepsilon\}}f^{p}\geq\varepsilon^{p}|\{f>\varepsilon\}|.

Hence,

\int(u_{0}-\varepsilon)_{+}\leq\frac{\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}}{\varepsilon^{p-1}}.

In particular,
(u_{0}-\varepsilon)_{+}\in L^{1}
as long as
u_{0}\in L^{p}
.

The comparison principle for sign-changing solutions of (
GPME
) with nonlinearity
\varphi
then gives

u_{0}(x)-\varepsilon\leq(u_{0}(x)-\varepsilon)_{+}\qquad\implies\qquad v_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\leq\tilde{v}_{\varepsilon}(x,t).

From which we conclude that

u(x,t)\leq\tilde{v}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)+\varepsilon\qquad\implies\qquad\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\tilde{v}_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\varepsilon.

We are then in the setting of Lemma
B.4
, and, for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\tilde{v}_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\varepsilon\leq F(t)\|(u_{0}-\varepsilon)_{+}\|_{L^{1}}^{\gamma}+c\varepsilon\leq F(t)\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}}^{\gamma p}\varepsilon^{-\gamma(p-1)}+c\varepsilon.

To conclude, we infimize over
\varepsilon>0
.
∎

Appendix C
Densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

The existence proof for weak dual solutions is based on the concept of
mild
solutions, which again relies on finding a.e.-solutions (!) of the corresponding elliptic problem

\forall\lambda>0\qquad u+\lambda A[u^{m}]=f\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

We will therefore study so-called
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive operators
A
.

C.1.
The setting of abstract solutions

The Laplacian
(-\Delta)
(as well as
r\mapsto(-\Delta)[r^{m}]
) is a well-known example of an operator which is
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

[
114
, Section 10.3.2]
. Now, we want to establish that any
symmetric, nonlocal
and
constant coefficient
Lévy operator

(-\mathcal{L}^{\mu})[\psi]=-P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}}\big(\psi(x+z)-\psi(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(z)

is also
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Since that operator is moreover
Dirichlet
, we get by Propositions 1 and 2 in
[
52
]
, that
r\mapsto(-\mathcal{L}^{\mu})[r^{m}]
is also
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Indeed, such a result should be well-known, but we did not manage to find a useful reference for it.

Throughout this section, we stick to the usual notation
A:=(-\mathcal{L}^{\mu})
.

Theorem C.1
.

Assume (
1
). Then the linear operator
A:D(A)\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
satisfies:

(i)

\overline{D(A)}^{\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}}=L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

(ii)

A
is accretive in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

(iii)

R(I+\lambda A)=L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for all
\lambda>0
.

(iv)

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a\leq f\leq b
a.e., then
a\leq(I+\lambda A)^{-1}f\leq b
a.e.

That is, the linear operator
A
is densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Remark C.2
.

(a)

Since
D(A)\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we define

D(A):=\{\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\,:\,\widetilde{A}[\psi]\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\}

where
\widetilde{A}
is the extension of
A
to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
(see (
C.2
) below). Moreover, in our case it is well-known that

A:C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\qquad\text{for all $p\in[1,\infty]$}.

Hence,
C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\subset D(A)
, and thus, we have already proven Theorem
C.1
(
i
). However, we need to make sure that when we define the extension
\widetilde{A}
(see (
C.2
) below), we have

\widetilde{A}|_{C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=A\qquad\text{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

(b)

In our
\mathbb{R}^{N}
-case, the numbers
a,b
in Theorem
C.1
(
iv
) has the natural restriction
a\leq b
,
a\leq 0
, and
b\geq 0
.

Corollary C.3

(
[
52
, Propositions 1 and 2]
)
.

Assume (
1
) and (

H
m

). Then the nonlinear operator
r\mapsto Ar^{m}:D(Ar^{m})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

In particular, for all
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that
a\leq f\leq b
, there exists a unique
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
which satisfies
a\leq u\leq b
, the comparison principle,
L^{p}
-decay estimate, and

u+\lambda\widetilde{A}[u^{m}]=f\qquad\text{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

Let us start by proving the range condition. To do so, consider

(C.1)

\forall\lambda>0\qquad u+\lambda A[u]=f\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$}.

Definition C.1

(Very weak solutions)
.

Assume (
1
). We say that
u\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a
very weak solution
of (
C.1
) with right-hand side
f\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
if

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u\psi\,\mathrm{d}x+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}uA[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}\end{split}

We need the following result (take
u\mapsto\lambda u
for all
\lambda>0
and choose
\varepsilon=1/\lambda
):

Lemma C.4

(
[
63
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Assume (
1
).

(a)

If
f\in C_{\textup{b}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then there exists a unique classical solution
u\in C_{\textup{b}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
C.1
). Moreover, for each multiindex
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
,

\|D^{\alpha}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|D^{\alpha}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(b)

If
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then there exists a unique classical solution
u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
C.1
). Moreover,

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

(c)

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then there exists a unique classical solution
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
C.1
). Moreover,

\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Remark C.5
.

Theorem 6.15 in
[
63
]
gives comparison (or
T
-contraction) as well: If
f\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that
(f)^{+}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
u\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a very weak solution of (
C.1
), then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(u)^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(f)^{+}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Lemma C.6
.

Assume
(
1
)
,
p\in(1,\infty]
,
f\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Let
u
be the very weak solution of (
C.1
) with right-hand side
f
. Then

\lambda\|\widetilde{A}[u]\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 2\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

where the extension
\widetilde{A}:D(A)\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
satisfies

(C.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\widetilde{A}[u]\psi\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}uA[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}

The proof follows after an immediate consequence.

Corollary C.7
.

Assume
(
1
)
,
p\in(1,\infty]
,
f\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Then the equation (
C.1
) (with
A\mapsto\widetilde{A}
) holds a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Proof of Lemma
C.6
.

Definition
C.1
gives

\begin{split}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\widetilde{A}[u]\psi\,\mathrm{d}x=\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}uA[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(f-u)\psi\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Now, take
q\in[1,\infty)
such that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. We recall Theorem 2.14 in
[
94
]
,

\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\sup_{\|\psi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 1}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\phi(x)\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|,

to obtain, by the Hölder inequality,

\begin{split}\lambda\|\widetilde{A}[u]\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}&=\lambda\sup_{\|\psi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 1}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\widetilde{A}[u]\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|=\sup_{\|\psi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 1}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(f-u)\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\\
&\leq\sup_{\|\psi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 1}\bigg\{\|f-u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\|\psi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\bigg\}\leq\|f-u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\\
&\leq 2\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.\end{split}

The proof is complete.
∎

Another consequence is that we can also extend
\widetilde{A}
to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and then make sense of the equation in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. To do so, we follow
[
40
]
.

Corollary C.8
.

Assume
(
1
)
and
f\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Then

\lambda\|\widetilde{A}[u]\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq 2\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Proof.

Since
f\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, Lemma
C.4
(c) yields

\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Moreover, by Corollary
C.7
, equation (
C.1
) holds pointwise, i.e.,

\|f-\lambda\widetilde{A}[u]\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

The reverse triangle inequality then provides the result.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
C.1
(
iii
).

Proposition C.9
.

Assume (
1
). For all
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, there exists a very weak solution
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
C.1
) such that, for all
\lambda>0
,

u+\lambda\widetilde{A}[u]=f\qquad\text{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$,}

where
\widetilde{A}
is the extension to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of
A
defined through the relation (
C.2
).

Proof.

Take
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that
f_{n}\to f
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
as
n\to\infty
. By Lemma
C.4
(c),

\|u_{n}-u_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|f_{n}-f_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Hence,
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and there exists a
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. In a similar way, through Corollary
C.8
,
\{\widetilde{A}[u_{n}]\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and there exists a
U\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that
\widetilde{A}[u_{n}]\to U
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. The definition of
\widetilde{A}
(
C.2
) then yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\widetilde{A}[u_{n}]\psi\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{n}A[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$}.

Moreover, since
\psi,A[\psi]\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, the
L^{1}
-convergence gives
U=\widetilde{A}[u]
. Finally, we take the
L^{1}
-limit in Definition
C.1
and use that fact that all the terms of the equation are elements in
L^{1}\subset L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}
.
∎

Remark C.10
.

In the literature, the property

u_{n}\to u\quad\text{in $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$}\qquad\implies\qquad\widetilde{A}[u_{n}]\to\widetilde{A}[u]\quad\text{in $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$}

is referred to as the operator
A
being
closed
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Here it automatically follows by the symmetry of the operator and that
A:C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Theorem
C.1
(
ii
) is a consequence of the
L^{1}
-contraction obtained in Lemma
C.4
(c) and Proposition
C.9
since then

(C.3)

\|(I+\lambda\widetilde{A})[u]\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\geq\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

We are then in the setting of the classical result:

Proposition C.11

(Hille-Yosida/Lumer-Phillips
[
82
, Theorem 4.1.33]
)
.

A linear operator
(A,D(A))
on
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is the generator of a strongly continuous contraction semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
if and only if
A
satisfies Theorem
C.1
(
i
)–(
iii
).

We, moreover, have that our operators are
maximal accretive
, i.e.:

Proposition C.12

(
[
12
, Proposition 8.3]
)
.

If
A
is
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, then
\widetilde{A}=A
.

Remark C.13
.

Hence, a posteriori,
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\ni u\mapsto A[u]
can be identified in a unique way as a limit point in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. See also Theorem 4.1.40 in
[
82
]
.

Our next task is Theorem
C.1
(
iv
), which follows by:

Proposition C.14
.

Assume (
1
) and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a\leq b
,
a\leq 0
, and
b\geq 0
. For all
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
such that

a\leq f\leq b\qquad\text{a.e.,}

the unique very weak solution
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
C.1
) satisfies

a\leq u\leq b\qquad\text{a.e.}

Proof.

By Remark
C.5
, the
T
-contraction holds for
L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}
-very weak solutions of (
C.1
). On one hand,
f\leq b
yields
(f-b)^{+}=0\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and then the
T
-contraction gives
u\leq b
. On the other hand,
a\leq f
yields
(a-f)^{+}=0\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and then the
T
-contraction gives
a\leq u
.

Here, we used that
a,b
are very weak solutions with
a,b
as right-hand side, and that bounded very weak solutions are unique.
∎

We have then proven that our operator
A
satisfies Theorem
C.1
(
i
)–(
iv
), which is exactly the setting of
[
52
]
.

Finally, let us recall why the above works for our operator
A=(-\mathcal{L}^{\mu})
. To deduce the
L^{1}
-contraction—or rather the
T
-contraction—needed to obtain (
C.3
), we employed a more fundamental result:

Lemma C.15
.

Assume (
1
). For all
u\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\widetilde{A}[u]\sgn^{+}(u)\,\mathrm{d}x\geq 0.

Remark C.16
.

This implies the condition stated as Corollary A.13 in
[
2
]
or in Proposition 4.6.12 in
[
82
]
(see also
[
103
]
for
p=1
) from which it follows that
A
is accretive.

Proof.

Remark
C.2
(a) gives
\widetilde{A}=A
. By a convex inequality,

A[u]\sgn^{+}(u)\geq A[(u)^{+}]\qquad\textup{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

Now, multiply each side by a smooth cut-off function
\mathcal{X}_{R}\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
satisfying
0\leq\mathcal{X}_{R}\leq 1
and
\mathcal{X}_{R}\to 1
pointwise as
R\to\infty
, integrate, use symmetry, and that
A[\mathcal{X}_{R}]\to 0
pointwise as
R\to\infty
.
∎

Corollary C.17
.

Assume (
1
). For all
u\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\widetilde{A}[u]\sgn^{+}(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}x\geq 0.

Remark C.18
.

Operators satisfying the above are called Dirichlet operators, see Definition 4.6.7 in
[
82
]
(and also
[
103
]
for
p=1
). Moreover, according to Proposition 4.6.9 in
[
82
]
Dirichlet operators imply Theorem
C.1
(
iv
) for both the semigroup and the resolvent of the semigroup generated by the operator, i.e., the semigroup and the resolvent of the semigroup are
sub-Markovian
.

Proof.

The result can be found as Corollary 3.2 in
[
103
]
, which actually provides an equivalence between the two conditions in our setting. Let us include a proof for completeness.

Again, Remark
C.2
(a) gives
\widetilde{A}=A
.
Now, take
u-\mathcal{X}_{R}\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
in Lemma
C.15
. Since
\sgn^{+}(u-1)\leq\sgn^{+}(u-\mathcal{X}_{R})
, we have that

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}A[u]\sgn^{+}(u-\mathcal{X}_{R})\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}A[\mathcal{X}_{R}]\sgn^{+}(u-\mathcal{X}_{R})\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}A[\mathcal{X}_{R}]\sgn^{+}(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Moreover,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\sgn^{+}(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\{u>1\}}1\,\mathrm{d}x<\int_{\{u>1\}}u\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|u|\,\mathrm{d}x,

which means that we can, again, use that
A[\mathcal{X}_{R}]\to 0
pointwise as
R\to\infty
on the right-hand side. While on the left-hand side we simply use the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Remark C.19
.

Lemma
C.4
is also true for the general operator (possibly local, nonlocal, or a combination)
\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}
defined by (
1.1
) with
c=0
, see
[
62
]
. Moreover, if
u
solves

\varepsilon u+(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})[u]=f\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ for all $\varepsilon>0$,}

then
u
solves

(\varepsilon-1)u+(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})[u]=f\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ for all $\varepsilon>1$.}

Now, take
u\mapsto\lambda u
and choose
\varepsilon=1+1/\lambda
to obtain that
u
solves

u+\lambda(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})[u]=f\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ for all $\lambda>0$.}

Hence,
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
and
(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
are also
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive. The latter is exactly (
1.1
) with
c=1
.

To prove the Dirichlet property, we used that
A[\textup{const}]=0
in Corollary
C.14
. This is of course true for
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
, while for
(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
, we have that
f=\textup{const}
gives
u=\textup{const}/(1+\lambda)
as a solution. Arguing as before, however, we still have
f\geq 0
(resp.
\leq 0
) implies
u\geq 0
(resp.
\leq 0
). According to Remark
C.2
, it remains to check
b>0,a<0
and
a\leq f\leq b
: We readily get
a/(1+\lambda)\leq u\leq b/(1+\lambda)
, i.e.,
a\leq u\leq b
since
\lambda>0
.

We then conclude that both
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
and
(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
satisfy Theorem
C.1
, Corollary
C.3
, and Proposition
C.12
. So, indeed the whole class of
symmetric
Lévy operators with
constant coefficients
are within the above framework.

C.2.
The setting of very weak solutions

We will now provide an a priori different approach to the one developed in the previous subsection.

Very weak solutions of

(C.4)

\forall\lambda>0\qquad u+\lambda A[u^{m}]=f\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$}

can be given as:

Definition C.2

(Very weak solutions)
.

Assume (
1
). We say that
u\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a
very weak solution
of (
C.4
) with right-hand side
f\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
if
u^{m}\in L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u\psi\,\mathrm{d}x+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u^{m}A[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}\end{split}

We collect uniqueness from
[
62
, Theorem 3.2]
, and a priori estimates from
[
65
, Remark 5.10]
.

Theorem C.20
.

Assume
0\leq f\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

), (
1
), and
A=(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
.

(a)

There exists a unique very weak solution
0\leq u\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
of (
C.4
) with right-hand side
f
.

(b)

Let
u,v
be two very weak solutions of (
C.4
) with respective right-hand sides
f,g
. Then:

(i)

(Comparison)
If
f\leq g
, then
u\leq v
.

(ii)

(
L^{p}
-decay)

\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
.

C.3.
Comparison between abstract and very weak solutions

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
only, it is hard to see how to construct very weak solutions of (
C.4
), but as in the abstract setting we could require that
a\leq f\leq b
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. We in any case have:

Lemma C.21
.

Assume
0\leq f\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, (

H
m

), and (
1
). If the operator
A
has an extension
\widetilde{A}
to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then very weak and a.e.-solutions of (
C.4
) coincide.

Remark C.22
.

Here we discover the advantage of
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive operators: By Proposition
C.12
,
\widetilde{A}=A
, and thus we obtain an a.e.-equation involving the operator itself! In addition, the abstract setting does not see the difference between
A=(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
and
A=(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
since they are both
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive.

Proof.

By Corollary
C.3
, we have a.e.-solutions of (
C.4
) (with
A\mapsto\widetilde{A}
). Multiplying by
\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and using the definition of the extension of the operator (
C.2
), shows that those a.e.-solutions are actually very weak solutions in the sense of Definition
C.2
. However, we can also start with very weak solutions by Theorem
C.20
, use the
L^{1}
-extension of the operator (
C.2
), and see that the equation (with
A\mapsto\widetilde{A}
) actually holds a.e. Hence, the equivalence between the elliptic problems is settled.
∎

Appendix D
The inverse of a densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, Dirichlet operator

This section is devoted to showing that a densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, Dirichlet operator in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
has an inverse such that (
2
) holds, under (possibly) some additional assumptions on the heat kernel associated with the operator.

Consider a strongly continuous contraction semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
which is moreover sub-Markovian. The discussion before Definition 3.5.17 in
[
83
]
gives that the resolvent
(R_{\lambda})_{\lambda>0}
of the semigroup is well-defined for functions in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, i.e.,

R_{\lambda}[\psi]:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-\lambda t}T_{t}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}

Moreover, the Green (or potential) operator
G
associated with
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is defined as

G[\psi]:=\lim_{\lambda\to 0^{+}}R_{\lambda}\psi\qquad\text{for all $0\leq\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}

Definition D.1

(Transient)
.

A strongly continuous sub-Markovian contraction semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
which is moreover sub-Markovian is called
transient
if

G[\psi](x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}T_{t}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\qquad\text{for all $0\leq\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}

By Proposition
C.11
, Theorem
C.1
, and Remark
C.19
, the operators
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
and
(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
generate the respective strongly continuous contraction semigroups
(T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
and
(T_{t}^{I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
which are moreover sub-Markovian. Note that by uniqueness of strongly continuous semigroups (cf. Corollary 4.1.35 in
[
82
]
),
(T_{t}^{I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}=(\textup{e}^{-t}T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
. An immediate consequence of Example 3.5.30 in
[
83
]
is:

Lemma D.1
.

The semigroup
(\textup{e}^{-t}T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
associated with
(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
, which satisfies Proposition
C.11
and Theorem
C.1
, is transient.

For the operator
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
, we note that the semigroup defined as

T_{t}[\psi]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\psi(x-y)\mathbb{H}_{t}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\qquad\textup{for all $\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$}

is a strongly continuous sub-Markovian contraction semigroup in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
with
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
as generator. This can easily be seen since
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
admits a symmetric and positive heat kernel satisfying
\mathbb{H}_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
due to the fact that the corresponding heat equation enjoys mass conservation,
L^{1}
-decay, the comparison principle, and has solutions in
C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
. By Corollary 4.1.35 in
[
82
]
, again, this semigroup must coincide with
(T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
. Moreover:

Lemma D.2

(
[
83
, Theorem 3.5.51]
)
.

The semigroup
(T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
associated with
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
, which satisfies Proposition
C.11
and Theorem
C.1
, is transient if and only if, for all compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{K}\mathbb{H}_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

At this point
G
is a good candidate for
A^{-1}
, but we need additional properties like e.g.
G:L^{p}\to D(A)
. The rigorous answer can be found in Proposition 3.5.79 in
[
83
]
. We simply check that

\lim_{t\to\infty}T_{t}[\psi]\to 0\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.}

For convolution semigroups with a symmetric positive kernel
\mathbb{H}_{t}
, we get

|T_{t}[\psi](x)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|\psi(x-y)|\mathbb{H}_{t}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

and hence, this is really a condition on the kernel by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem. Note that we can immediately conclude for
I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}
since
T_{t}^{I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}[\psi]=\textup{e}^{-t}T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}[\psi]
,
\|T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}[\psi]\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}
, and hence, we obtain the stronger property

\textup{e}^{-t}T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}[\psi]\to 0\qquad\text{in $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ as $t\to\infty$.}

The above can then be summarized as:

Proposition D.3

(The inverse operator
A^{-1}
)
.

(a)

The semigroup
(\textup{e}^{-t}T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
associated with
(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
, which satisfies Proposition
C.11
and Theorem
C.1
, has an inverse operator given, for all
0\leq\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, by

(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})^{-1}[\psi]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}t,

and
(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})^{-1}[(I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})[\psi]]=\psi
.

(b)

Assume that the semigroup
(T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}})_{t\geq 0}
associated with
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
, which satisfies Proposition
C.11
and Theorem
C.1
, has a symmetric and positive kernel
\mathbb{H}_{t}
satisfying, for all compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{K}\mathbb{H}_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{H}_{t}(x)\to 0\quad\text{for a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

Then
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})
has an inverse operator given, for all
0\leq\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, by

(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})^{-1}[\psi]=\int_{0}^{\infty}T_{t}^{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{t}(y)\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg)\psi(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

and
(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})^{-1}[(-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu})[\psi]]=\psi
.

Remark D.4
.

(a)

We immediately see that, in the setting of part (b) above,

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{t}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Moreover, if we apply the setting of part (b) to part (a), then we also have that

\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}^{\sigma,\mu}}^{x_{0}}(x):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{e}^{-t}\mathbb{H}_{t}^{x_{0}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Of course, the latter is well-defined for a larger class of kernels than the former.

(b)

We can then check that all the examples considered in Section
7
have inverses.

The positivity assumption of (
2
) is then very natural since it is related to the fact that the operator ensures the comparison principle.

Corollary D.5
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
D.3
,

\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x),\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\geq 0\qquad\text{for a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

Proof.

Since the comparison principle holds for solutions of the heat equation,
\mathbb{H}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x,t)\geq 0
.
∎

In fact, we only need to assume that the resolvent is nonnegative since
\textup{e}^{-t}\leq 1
:

Corollary D.6
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
D.3
,

0\leq\mathbb{G}_{I-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\leq\mathbb{G}_{-\mathfrak{L}}^{x_{0}}(x)\qquad\text{for a.e. $x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$.}

Appendix E
Existence and a priori results for weak dual solutions

Although this is not the main point of the paper, we will illustrate that our assumptions (
2
)–(
2
) does not lead to an empty theory. Let us therefore prove Proposition
4.1
. To do so, we rely on the theory of abstract solutions for the corresponding elliptic problem of (
GPME
). Recall Lemma
4.8
, and due to (
2
), we also have:

Lemma E.1
.

Consider
A:=(-\mathfrak{L})
or
A:=(I-\mathfrak{L})
. Then
A[\mathbb{G}_{A}^{y}]=\delta_{y}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,

A^{-1}[\psi]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathbb{G}_{A}^{0}(x-y)\psi(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

and
A[A^{-1}[\psi]]=\psi
for all
\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

(a) Consider a uniform grid in time such that
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{J}=T
. Let
\mathbb{J}:=\{1,\ldots,J\}
, and denote the time steps by
\Delta t=t_{j}-t_{j-1}
for all
j\in\mathbb{J}
. The piecewise constant time interpolant
u_{\Delta t}
is, for
(x,t)\in Q_{T}
, given as

u_{\Delta t}(x,t):=u_{j}(x)\quad\text{where $t\in(t_{j-1},t_{j}]$ for all $j\in\mathbb{J}$,}

and
u_{\Delta t}(x,0):=u_{0}(x)
. Now, each
u_{j}
is defined recursively as the solution of the following elliptic equation:

u_{j}+\Delta tA[u_{j}^{m}]=u_{j-1}\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$,}

which of course is another way of expressing (
C.4
). Since
A
is densely defined,
\mathfrak{m}
-accretive, and Dirichlet in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, the above equation has an a.e.-solution (cf. Theorem
C.1
and Proposition
C.12
). Then rewriting the equation, multiplying by
A^{-1}[\psi(\cdot,t_{j-1})]
with
\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(0,T))
, integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, using

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}A[u_{j}^{m}]A^{-1}[\psi(\cdot,t_{j-1})]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{j}^{m}A[A^{-1}[\psi(\cdot,t_{j-1})]]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{j}^{m}\psi(\cdot,t_{j-1}),

and summing over
j
, we obtain that

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{J}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{u_{j}-u_{j-1}}{\Delta t}A^{-1}[\psi(t_{j-1})]\,\mathrm{d}x\Delta t+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{J}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{j}^{m}\psi(t_{j-1})\,\mathrm{d}x\Delta t=0.

We now perform summation by parts, use the symmetry of
A^{-1}
, and use that
\psi
has compact support in
(0,T)
(so that it vanishes for small enough
\Delta t
for some
n,m\in\mathbb{J}
) to obtain

-\sum_{j=n}^{m-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}A^{-1}[u_{j}]\frac{\psi(t_{j})-\psi(t_{j-1})}{\Delta t}\,\mathrm{d}x\Delta t+\sum_{j=n}^{m-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u_{j}^{m}\psi(t_{j-1})\,\mathrm{d}x\Delta t=0.

At this point, we can follow the proof of Proposition 5.2 in
[
15
]
since, in our case, we have that
A^{-1}[u_{j}]\in C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))\cap L^{\infty}(Q_{T})
and e.g.

|u_{j}^{m}-u^{m}(t_{j})|\leq 2\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{m-1}|u_{j}-u(t_{j})|.

That is,
u\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(Q_{T})\cap C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
satisfies

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big(A^{-1}[u]\partial_{t}\psi-u^{m}\psi\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=0\qquad\text{for all $\psi\in C_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(Q_{T})$.}

Assume
0<\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq T
, and choose
\psi(x,t)\mapsto\theta_{n}(t)\psi(x,t)
where the new
\psi
is in
C_{\textup{c}}^{1}([\tau_{1},\tau_{2}];L_{\textup{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
and
\theta_{n}
is an approximation of the square pulse with support in
[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]
. The above expression is still well-defined for this choice, and moreover, since e.g.
A^{-1}[u]\in C([0,T];L_{\textup{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
, we can take the limit as
n\to\infty
. This concludes that
u
is a weak dual solution according to Definition
2.1
.

(b) The comparison principle and the
L^{p}
-decay are immediately inherited from the elliptic problem, see e.g. the proofs of Propositions 5.1 and 5.2 in
[
15
]
.
∎

Remark E.2
.

We have in fact shown that mild/integral solutions (i.e., the limit points of the time-discretized problem) of (
GPME
) are weak dual solutions.
